## F001

**Question:** In Figure 1B-1, what happens immediately after FHWA approves a request to experiment?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1C.02, Section 1B.05, Figure 1B-1, Figure 1B-2, p.7, p.8. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The requesting jurisdiction installs the experimental traffic control device. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=1.000. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: In Figure 1B-1, once FHWA approves the request (the "Yes" branch of the "Approved?" decision), the very next step is that the requesting jurisdiction installs the experimental traffic control device. From there the process branches into two parallel activities—evaluating the experimental device and providing semi-annual reports to FHWA Division & HQ—both of which lead to the requesting jurisdiction providing FHWA a copy of the final report. Standards (mandatory): - Context for the figure: "A traffic control device or application that does not comply with the provisions of this Manual shall not be used on any street, highway, bikeway, or site roadway open to public travel (see definition in Section 1C.02) without first receiving official approval to experiment from the FHWA's Office of Transportation Operations." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 1B-1 shows the "Yes" arrow from the "Approved?" diamond leading directly to the box "Requesting jurisdiction installs experimental traffic control device," which then splits into "Evaluate experimental traffic control device" and "Requesting jurisdiction provides semi-annual reports to FHWA Division & HQ," both converging on "Requesting jurisdiction provides FHWA a copy of final report." - [Image 2] The full page view of the figure page confirms the same sequence: approval ("Yes") flows to installation of the experimental device, followed by the evaluation and semi-annual reporting steps and the final report. - [Image 3] This page (Figure 1B-2) shows the separate downstream process for incorporating a device into the MUTCD after successful experimentation, which follows rather than precedes the Figure 1B-1 steps. - [Image 4] This page provides text context (Sections 1B.06 and 1B.07) referencing Figures 1B-1 and 1B-2 but does not alter the sequence shown in Figure 1B-1. - [Image 5] This page lists the mandatory contents of an experimentation request, including agreements for semi-annual reports and a final report that correspond to the post-approval steps shown in the figure. Citations: - Figure 1B-1 (p.7) - Section 1B.05 Support §9 (p.8) - Section 1B.05 Standard §2 (p.6) - Page 48

**Gold answer:** The requesting jurisdiction installs the experimental traffic control device.

## F002

**Question:** In Figure 1B-2, if a proposed change is accepted for Federal rulemaking, what process follows before a Final Rule is published?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1B.07, Section 1B.06, Section 1B.02, Figure 1B-2, Page 11, Page 9. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: FHWA develops provisions for a Notice of Proposed Amendments, publishes the notice in the Federal Register, holds a docket comment period, reviews comments, prepares the Final Rul… Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.944 and Context Precision@6=0.667. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=0.00, visual recall=0.00, and visual precision=0.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: According to Figure 1B-2, once a proposed change is accepted for Federal rulemaking, FHWA develops the device provisions for inclusion in the next Notice of Proposed Amendments, publishes that Notice in the Federal Register, holds a docket comment period, reviews the comments received, and then prepares the Final Rule before publishing it. In parallel, the change may also be considered for interim approval, allowing optional use of the device pending completion of the rulemaking. After the Final Rule is published, State manuals must be brought into substantial conformance within 2 years. Standards (mandatory): - "States or other Federal agencies that have their own MUTCDs or Supplements shall revise these MUTCDs or Supplements to be in substantial conformance with changes to the National MUTCD within 2 years of the effective date of the Final Rule for the changes [23 CFR 655.603(b)(3)]." Options (permitted): - Interim approval may run in parallel with rulemaking: "Interim approval allows for the optional use of a traffic control device or application and does not create a new mandate or recommendation for its use," with jurisdictions agreeing to conditions "until an official rulemaking action has occurred." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 1B-2 shows the "YES" branch from "Accepted for Federal rulemaking?" flowing through: FHWA develops device provisions for inclusion in the next Notice of Proposed Amendments → FHWA publishes Notice of Proposed Amendments in Federal Register → Docket comment period → FHWA reviews comments → FHWA prepares Final Rule → FHWA publishes Final Rule, with a parallel "Interim approval?" decision leading to interim approval issuance and deployment. [Image 3] Page 11 explains that a device under interim approval might be proposed for adoption in the next scheduled rulemaking, and if not, its status will be stated in the Federal Register notice. [Image 4] Page 9 states that the process for incorporating new traffic control devices into the Manual is shown in Figure 1B-2. Citations: - Figure 1B-2 (p.9) - Section 1B.07 Support §3 (p.11) - Section 1B.07 Support §4 (p.11) - Section 1B.06 Support §4 (p.9) - Section 1B.02 Standard §2 (p.4)

**Gold answer:** FHWA develops provisions for a Notice of Proposed Amendments, publishes the notice in the Federal Register, holds a docket comment period, reviews comments, prepares the Final Rule, and publishes it.

## F004

**Question:** In Figure 2A-2, what minimum mounting height and lateral offset are shown for a basic roadside sign in a rural area without a wider shoulder?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2A.15, Section 2A.16, Figure 2A-2, p.58, p.60, p.51. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: A 5-foot minimum mounting height and a 12-foot minimum lateral offset from the edge of the traveled way. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.667. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: For a basic roadside sign in a rural area without a shoulder wider than 6 feet (Panel A of Figure 2A-2), the figure shows a minimum mounting height of 5 feet, measured from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement, and a minimum lateral offset of 12 feet from the edge of the traveled way. (The 6-foot offset from the edge of shoulder in Panel B applies only where a shoulder wider than 6 feet exists.) Standards (mandatory): - "In rural areas, the minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement, of signs installed at the side of the road shall be 5 feet (see Figure 2A-2)." Guidance (recommended): - "For post-mounted signs, the minimum lateral offset should be 12 feet from the edge of the traveled way. If a shoulder wider than 6 feet exists, the minimum lateral offset for post-mounted signs should be 6 feet from the edge of the shoulder." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 2A-2, Panel A ("Roadside sign in rural area"), depicts a STOP sign with "5 ft MIN." height above the near edge of the pavement and "12 ft MIN." lateral offset from the edge of the traveled way, while Panel B shows the alternative 6-foot offset only where the shoulder is wider than 6 feet. Citations: - Section 2A.15 Standard §4 (p.58) - Section 2A.16 Guidance §3 (p.60) - Figure 2A-2 (p.51)

**Gold answer:** A 5-foot minimum mounting height and a 12-foot minimum lateral offset from the edge of the traveled way.

Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The distinguishing visual feature is whether the blue paths of the opposing left-turning vehicles cross each other within the median. In Panel A ("Separate intersections"), the median is wide enough that the two opposing left-turn paths overlap and cross one another; in Panel B ("Single intersection"), the median is narrower and the opposing left-turn paths curve past each other without crossing. Guidance (recommended): - A divided highway crossing should be signed and marked as separate intersections when both of the following conditions are present: "A. The paths of opposing left turns from the divided highway cross each other (see Figure 2A-5), and B. There is adequate storage in the interior approaches for the design vehicles expected to cross the divided highway." - The figure's panel captions reflect this distinction: "A - Separate intersections (paths of opposing left-turning vehicles cross)" and "B - Single intersection (paths of opposing left-turning vehicles do not cross)." At the crossing of two divided highways, engineering judgment should be used to determine the number of separate intersections. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2A-5 shows Panel A with a wide median where the two blue left-turn vehicle paths cross each other inside the median, and Panel B with a narrower median where the blue left-turn paths do not intersect. - [Image 2] The full-page view of Figure 2A-5 confirms the panel titles explicitly stating that opposing left-turn paths cross in the separate-intersections case and do not cross in the single-intersection case. - [Image 3] Page 93 shows Figure 2A-3 (sign locations at intersections) and is not relevant to this distinction. - [Image 4] Page 1145 shows two-stage bicycle turn box locations and is not relevant to this distinction. - [Image 5] Page 588 shows two-way left-turn lane markings and is not relevant to this distinction. Citations: - Section 2A.23 Guidance §1 (p.63) - Section 2A.23 Guidance §3 (p.64) - Figure 2A-5 (p.64)

**Gold answer:** In the separate-intersections case, the paths of opposing left-turning vehicles cross; in the single-intersection case, those paths do not cross.

The ALL-WAY (R1-3P) plaque has a white legend and border on a red background. The ALL-WAY plaque shall have a white legend and border on a red background." - "When it is determined that a full stop is always required on an approach to an intersection, a STOP (R1-1) sign (see Figure 2B-1) shall be used." - "The YIELD (R1-2) sign (see Figure 2B-1) shall not be displayed using a changeable message sign." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2B-1 shows the STOP (R1-1) sign as a red octagon with white legend and border, the YIELD (R1-2) sign as a downward-pointing triangle with red border and white interior, and the ALL-WAY (R1-3P) plaque as a red rectangle with white "ALL WAY" legend and white border. - [Image 2] Page 74 (full page view) presents Figure 2B-1 in context, confirming the octagonal STOP sign, triangular YIELD sign, and red ALL-WAY plaque with white legend, along with the white rectangular supplemental plaques (R1-2aP, R1-2bP, R1-2cP, R1-10P). - [Image 3] Page 974 shows school-crossing sign assemblies (R1-5a, R1-5c, R1-6 series) that reproduce the same octagonal STOP and triangular YIELD symbol shapes. - [Image 4] Page 794 shows the STOP (R1-1) octagon and YIELD (R1-2) triangle among regulatory signs used in temporary traffic control zones, consistent with Figure 2B-1. - [Image 5] Page 118 shows the octagonal STOP sign in an intersection signing layout (Figure 2B-20), consistent with the shape depicted in Figure 2B-1. Citations: - Section 2B.04 Standard §4 (p.74) - Section 2B.04 Standard §1 (p.74) - Section 2B.05 Standard §2 (p.75) - Figure 2B-1 (p.74)

**Gold answer:** STOP is an octagon; YIELD is a downward-pointing equilateral triangle; the ALL-WAY plaque has white legend and border on a red background.

The buffer interval shall not begin later than the beginning of the red clearance interval, if used." Options (permitted): - Per the figure's footnotes: "The countdown display is optional for Pedestrian Change Intervals of 7 seconds or less"; "The Walk Interval may be reduced under some conditions (see Section 4I.06)"; and "The Buffer Interval, which shall always be provided and displayed, may be used to help satisfy the calculated pedestrian clearance time, or may begin after the calculated pedestrian clearance time has ended." - The additional time provided by an extended push button press may be added to either the walk interval or the pedestrian change interval. Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Figure 4I-4 with the interval sequence Walk Interval → Pedestrian Change Interval → Buffer Interval, with "7 seconds MIN.**" labeled under the Walk Interval and "2 seconds MIN." labeled for the Buffer Interval, along with the "'Zero' point of countdown display" occurring at the start of the Buffer Interval. Options (permitted): - Per the note in Figure 4J-3: "An optional steady red clearance interval may be added after Interval 3, and an optional short buffer interval (alternating flashing red while the pedestrian signal heads are displaying a steady UPRAISED HAND) may be added after Interval 5." This four-area division is informational (Support), applied as needed based on engineering judgment rather than as a mandatory requirement. Standards (mandatory): (none — the four-area division is presented as Support, not a Standard) Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 6B-1 depicts, from bottom to top in the direction of travel (northbound), the Advance Warning Area ("tells traffic what to expect ahead"), the Transition Area ("moves traffic out of its normal path") containing the shoulder taper, the Activity Area ("is where work takes place") containing the work space, buffer spaces, and traffic space, and the Termination Area ("lets traffic resume normal operations") containing the downstream taper. Citations: - Section 6B.03 Support §1 (p.771) - Figure 6B-1 (p.772)

**Gold answer:** Advance warning area, transition area, activity area, and termination area.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The principal layout difference shown in Figure 8E-1 is the relationship to the roadway. In Panel A, the pathway occupies its own independent "Pathway right-of-way" and crosses the tracks with no adjacent roadway, whereas in Panel B the sidewalk runs parallel and immediately adjacent to a roadway within the "Highway right-of-way," so both cross the tracks together. This matters because roadway grade crossing traffic control devices can typically warn sidewalk users but not pathway users. Standards (mandatory): - "Detectable warnings (see Chapter 3C) shall be used at pathway grade crossings where pedestrian travel is permitted and at sidewalk grade crossings and shall extend across the full width of the pathway or sidewalk." (Both panels of Figure 8E-1 show detectable warnings on each approach.) Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 8E-1 shows Panel A with a stand-alone pathway crossing the railroad within its own labeled "Pathway right-of-way," and Panel B with a sidewalk directly alongside a roadway (with yellow centerline) inside a labeled "Highway right-of-way," with detectable warnings marked on both approaches in each panel. [Image 2] The full page confirms the accompanying Support text explaining that a pathway is frequently placed in its own right-of-way independent of any roadway, while a sidewalk runs parallel to a roadway within the highway right-of-way close to the traveled way. Citations: - Section 8E.01 Support §4 (p.1035) - Section 8E.04 Standard §7 (p.1038) - Figure 8E-1 (p.1035) - Page 1076

**Gold answer:** The pathway crossing is on a separate pathway alignment outside the roadway, whereas the sidewalk crossing is part of the highway corridor alongside the roadway and crosses the tracks with the roadway.

## M001

**Question:** A provision appears under a Guidance heading and uses the word 'should.' Is it mandatory, and when may a deviation be appropriate?

**Grade:** C=0.95, Comp=0.95, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=No

**Descriptive mean:** 97.5% (Strong partial credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 76%

**Rationale:** The response received 97.5% as the descriptive mean of the four official item scores. The official rubric did not award strict full credit because at least one dimension scored below 1.00. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response gives the main conclusion but loses precision on one or more required details. Completeness rationale: The audit found incomplete coverage even though the automated clause comparison did not isolate a single missing sentence. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1D.03, Section 1C.01, Section 2D.06, Section 6P.01, p.13, p.32. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: It is recommended practice, not a mandatory Standard. | A deviation may be appropriate when engineering judgment or an engineering study indicates that the deviation is suitable for the specific situation. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No, a Guidance provision is not mandatory. By definition, Guidance is a statement of recommended practice in typical situations, and deviations from it are allowed when engineering judgment or an engineering study indicates the deviation to be appropriate. This differs from a Standard, which is a required or prohibitive practice from which deviation is possible only in limited, location-specific cases supported by a documented engineering study. Standards (mandatory): - "Guidance-a statement of recommended practice in typical situations, with deviations allowed if engineering judgment or engineering study (see Section 1D.03) indicates the deviation to be appropriate. All Guidance statements are labeled, and the text appears in unbold italic type. The verb 'should' is typically used. The verbs 'shall' and 'may' are not used in Guidance statements. Guidance statements are sometimes modified by Option statements." - By contrast: "Standard-a statement of required, mandatory, or specifically prohibitive practice regarding a traffic control device. In limited, location-specific cases, the results of a documented engineering study (see Section 1D.03) might indicate a deviation from one or more requirements of a Standard provision to be appropriate." Guidance (recommended): - As an illustration of a typical Guidance provision using "should": "Except where otherwise provided in this Manual, guide signs should be limited to no more than three lines of destinations…" — a recommended practice from which deviation is permitted where engineering judgment or study supports it. Support (contextual, non-normative): - The application of engineering study and engineering judgment is a fundamental principle of the use of traffic control devices; in most cases the selection of a particular device is determined by engineering study or engineering judgment rather than required by a Standard, and even deviations from Standards are allowed only in limited, specific cases when fully documented through an engineering study. - In the temporary traffic control typical applications, except for notes that are explicitly labeled, the information presented can generally be regarded as Guidance — meaning it, too, is recommended rather than mandatory. Citations: - Section 1C.01 Standard §1 (p.13) - Section 1D.03 Support §2 (p.32) - Section 2D.06 Guidance §2 (p.209) - Section 6P.01 Support §2 (p.857)

**Gold answer:** It is recommended practice, not a mandatory Standard. A deviation may be appropriate when engineering judgment or an engineering study indicates that the deviation is suitable for the specific situation.

The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Using a retroreflective strip on a sign support is a permitted conspicuity-enhancement method chosen by engineering judgment, but once used it must meet mandatory dimensions and placement. The strip must be at least 2 inches wide, run the full length of the support from the sign to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway, match the sign's background color (red for YIELD and DO NOT ENTER signs), and carry no legend. A related mandatory application exists for Crossbuck supports at passive grade crossings, where a white retroreflective strip is required on the back of each support. Standards (mandatory): - "If a strip of retroreflective material is used on the sign support, it shall be at least 2 inches in width, it shall be placed for the full length of the support from the sign to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway, and its color shall match the background color of the sign, except that the color of the strip for the YIELD and DO NOT ENTER signs shall be red. The retroreflective strip shall not display any legend or other information." - For passive grade crossings: "A vertical strip of retroreflective white material, not less than 2 inches in width, shall be used on each Crossbuck support at passive grade crossings for the full length of the back of the support from the Crossbuck sign or Number of Tracks plaque to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway... A white retroreflective strip wrapped around a round support... shall satisfy this requirement as long as the round support has an outside diameter of at least 2 inches." Options (permitted): - Based on engineering judgment, where improvement of the conspicuity of a standard regulatory, warning, or guide sign is desired, adding a strip of retroreflective material to the sign support (see Figure 2A-1) may be used as one of the listed conspicuity-enhancement methods. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2A-1, panel E ("Vertical retroreflective strip on sign support"), depicts a fluorescent yellow-green strip matching the pedestrian sign's background color running down the support and terminating with a "2 ft MAX." dimension above the near edge of the roadway. - [Image 2] The full-page view of Figure 2A-1 (p.48) confirms the strip is one of seven illustrated conspicuity-enhancement examples (A–G) referenced in Section 2A.11. - [Image 3] The page containing Section 2A.21 notes that "Information regarding the use of retroreflective material on the sign support is contained in Section 2A.11." - [Image 4] The page containing Section 2A.11 shows the Standard text (Paragraph 5) with the 2-inch width, full-length placement, 2-foot termination, color-matching, and no-legend requirements. - [Image 5] Figure 8B-2 illustrates the Crossbuck Assembly application, showing a "2-inch white retroreflective strip on back of support," an "Optional 2-inch red or white retroreflective strip on front of support," and the "2 ft MAX." termination above the edge of roadway. Citations: - Section 2A.11 Standard §5 (p.49) - Section 2A.11 Option §1 (p.49) - Section 2A.21 Support §8 (p.62) - Section 8B.04 Standard §17 (p.1000) - Figure 2A-1 (p.48) - Page 89 - Page 103 - Page 90 - Page 1038

**Gold answer:** The strip must be at least 2 inches wide, extend from the sign down the full support to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway, match the sign background color except that YIELD and DO NOT ENTER strips are red, and contain no legend or other information.

Figure 2A-2 (drawings A and B) shows the corresponding normal lateral offset: a minimum of 12 feet from the edge of the traveled way, or a minimum of 6 feet from the edge of the shoulder where the shoulder is wider than 6 feet. Where such offsets are impractical, Section 2A.16 permits a reduced offset of at least 2 feet on certain roads. Standards (mandatory): - "In rural areas, the minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement, of signs installed at the side of the road shall be 5 feet (see Figure 2A-2)." - For context, "Where signs serve both bicyclists and other road users, vertical mounting height and lateral placement shall be as provided in Sections 2A.15 and 2A.16 of this manual." Guidance (recommended): - The figure material accompanying Section 2A.13 notes: "Lateral offset is a minimum of 6 feet measured from the edge of the shoulder, or 12 feet measured from the edge of the traveled way," and refers to Section 2A.16 for lower minimums where lateral offset space is limited. Options (permitted): - "On conventional, low-volume rural, and special-purpose roads in areas where it is impractical to locate a sign with the lateral offset prescribed by this Section because of roadside features such as terrain or vegetation, a lateral offset of at least 2 feet may be used." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2A-2, drawing A ("Roadside sign in rural area"), shows a STOP sign with "12 ft MIN." lateral offset from the traveled way and "5 ft MIN." mounting height; drawing B shows "6 ft MIN." from the edge of a shoulder wider than 6 feet with the same "5 ft MIN." height. - [Image 2] The full page view of Figure 2A-2 confirms the same rural dimensions (12 ft MIN. lateral offset, 5 ft MIN. height) and the note referring to Section 2A.16 for reduced lateral offsets. - [Image 3] Figure 2M-3 (recreational/cultural interest area signs) shows analogous rural assembly dimensions (12 ft MIN. offset, 5 ft MIN. height) but is not the primary evidence for a basic rural roadside sign. - [Image 4] The Section 2A.15/2A.16 text page shows the mounting-height provisions and the beginning of the Lateral Offset section supporting the offset requirements. - [Image 5] Figure 2A-3 carries the note that lateral offset is a minimum of 6 feet from the edge of the shoulder or 12 feet from the edge of the traveled way, with Section 2A.16 governing lower minimums. Citations: - Section 2A.15 Standard §4 (p.58) - Section 9A.02 Standard §4 (p.1047) - Section 2A.13 Guidance §6 (p.57) - Section 2A.16 Option §9 (p.60) - Figure 2A-2 (p.51) - Page 92 - Page 93 - Page 100

**Gold answer:** The minimum mounting height is 5 feet. The normal minimum lateral offset is 12 feet from the edge of the traveled way, or 6 feet from the edge of a shoulder wider than 6 feet.

Since abbreviations on CMS must comply with Section 1D.08, the designer cannot use DLY on a portable changeable message sign. Standards (mandatory): - "The abbreviations shown in Table 1D-3 shall not be used in connection with traffic control devices because of their potential to be misinterpreted by road users." - "Abbreviations displayed on CMS shall comply with the provisions of Section 1D.08." - "When the word messages shown in Table 1D-2 need to be abbreviated on a portable changeable message sign, the abbreviations shown in Table 1D-2 shall be used. Unless indicated by an asterisk, these abbreviations shall only be used on portable changeable message signs." (If "Delay" needs abbreviating, only an abbreviation approved in Table 1D-2 could be used — not DLY.) Guidance (recommended): - If a permissible alternative abbreviation exists in Table 1D-1 or 1D-2 with more than one allowed form, "the same abbreviation should be used throughout a single jurisdiction." Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Table 1D-3 (Unacceptable Abbreviations), which lists "DLY" with the intended word "Delay" and the common misinterpretation "Daily," confirming that DLY is expressly prohibited. **Gold answer:** No. DLY is explicitly prohibited because it can be misinterpreted as DAILY.

No, a 4-WAY plaque may not be substituted — plaques with legends such as 2-WAY, 3-WAY, or 4-WAY are expressly prohibited with STOP signs. Standards (mandatory): - "At intersections where all approaches are controlled by STOP signs (see Section 2B.12), an ALL-WAY (R1-3P) supplemental plaque (see Figure 2B-1) shall be mounted below each STOP sign. The ALL-WAY plaque shall have a white legend and border on a red background." - "Supplemental plaques with legends such as 2-WAY, 3-WAY, 4-WAY, or other numbers of ways shall not be used with STOP signs." - "When it is determined that a full stop is always required on an approach to an intersection, a STOP (R1-1) sign (see Figure 2B-1) shall be used." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 2B-1 shows the STOP (R1-1) sign and the ALL-WAY (R1-3P) plaque, which is depicted as a horizontal rectangular plaque with white "ALL WAY" legend and white border on a red background, alongside the YIELD (R1-2) sign and related plaques (R1-2aP, R1-2bP, R1-2cP, R1-10P). Citations: - Section 2B.04 Standard §4 (p.74) - Section 2B.04 Standard §5 (p.74) - Section 2B.04 Standard §1 (p.74) - Figure 2B-1 (p.74)

**Gold answer:** An ALL-WAY (R1-3P) plaque must be mounted below each STOP sign. It has a white legend and border on a red background. A 4-WAY or other numeric-way plaque may not be substituted.

**Question:** Compare the quantitative triggers in the four-leg All-Way Stop crash warrant and the 8-hour volume warrant.

Completeness rationale: The audit found incomplete coverage even though the automated clause comparison did not isolate a single missing sentence. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2B.13, Section 2B.16, p.79. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The crash warrant may apply with at least 5 correctable crashes in 12 months or 6 in 36 months. | The volume warrant may apply when, for the same 8 hours, major-street entering volume is at least 300 units/hour and minor-street entering volume at least 200 units/hour, with a p… Adjudication note: Correct crash and eight-hour volume thresholds; omits the 70-percent high-speed adjustment. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 2, which produced MRR=0.500. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.624 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Both warrants are Option provisions permitting all-way stop control based on an engineering study, but they use different measures. The crash warrant for a four-leg intersection is triggered by five or more reported crashes in 12 months, or six or more in 36 months, of a type correctable by all-way stop control. The 8-hour volume warrant instead requires combined vehicle/bicycle/pedestrian volumes of at least 300 units per hour entering from the major street and at least 200 units per hour from the minor street, during each of the same 8 hours of a typical day. Options (permitted): - Under Warrant A (Crash Experience), all-way stop control may be installed where an engineering study indicates that, "For a four-leg intersection, there are five or more reported crashes in a 12-month period or six or more reported crashes in a 36-month period that were of a type susceptible to correction by the installation of all-way stop control." - Under Warrant D (8-Hour Volume), all-way stop control may be installed where an engineering study indicates that "the combined motor vehicle, bicycle, and pedestrian volume entering the intersection from the major-street approaches is at least 300 units per hour for each of any 8 hours of a typical day," and "the combined motor vehicle, bicycle, and pedestrian volume entering the intersection from the minor-street approaches is at least 200 units per hour for each of any of the same 8 hours." Key quantitative comparison: - Crash warrant (four-leg): counts crash frequency — thresholds of 5 crashes/12 months or 6 crashes/36 months, limited to crash types correctable by all-way stop control. - 8-hour volume warrant: counts multimodal entering volume — thresholds of 300 units/hour (major street) and 200 units/hour (minor street), which must be met concurrently during the same 8 hours of a typical day. - The crash warrant is retrospective (based on reported crash history over 12- or 36-month periods), while the volume warrant is based on measured hourly demand that must be sustained for 8 hours. Citations: - Section 2B.13 Option §1 (p.79) - Section 2B.16 Option §1 (p.79)

**Gold answer:** The crash warrant may apply with at least 5 correctable crashes in 12 months or 6 in 36 months. The volume warrant may apply when, for the same 8 hours, major-street entering volume is at least 300 units/hour and minor-street entering volume at least 200 units/hour, with a possible 70-percent adjustment if major-street 85th-percentile speed exceeds 40 mph.

Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2A.22, Table 2A-5, p.62, p.63. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: For white on red, the table gives white at least 35, red at least 7 cd/lx/m², and at least a 3:1 white-to-red contrast ratio. | The agency complies by having and using a method designed to maintain the minimums, even if some individual signs fall below them at a particular moment. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.975 and Context Precision@6=0.833. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. Compliance with the Standard is achieved by having and using an assessment or management method designed to maintain the minimum levels in Table 2A-5; the MUTCD explicitly explains that an agency using such a method is still in compliance even if some individual signs are below the minimums at a particular point in time. Signs found by the agency's method to be below the minimums should, however, be replaced. For white-on-red (STOP-type) signs, Table 2A-5 requires a minimum maintained white retroreflectivity of 35 and red of 7 cd/lx/m², with a minimum white-to-red contrast ratio of at least 3:1. Standards (mandatory): - "Public agencies or officials having jurisdiction shall use an assessment or management method that is designed to maintain sign retroreflectivity at or above the minimum levels in Table 2A-5." - Table 2A-5 footnote: "Minimum sign contrast ratio ≥ 3:1 (white retroreflectivity ÷ red retroreflectivity)" applies to white-on-red signs. Guidance (recommended): - Except for signs identified in Paragraph 5, one or more of the methods described in "Maintaining Traffic Sign Retroreflectivity" (FHWA-SA-07-020, Revised 2013), FHWA, or a method developed based on an engineering study, should be used to maintain sign retroreflectivity at or above the minimum levels in Table 2A-5; "Signs that are identified through the agency's method as being below the minimum levels should be replaced." Options (permitted): - Highway agencies may exclude from the retroreflectivity maintenance guidelines: (A) Parking, Standing, and Stopping (R7 and R8 series) signs; (B) Walking/Hitchhiking/Crossing (R9 series, R10-1 through R10-4b) signs; (C) Acknowledgment signs; and (D) Bikeway signs intended for exclusive use by bicyclists or pedestrians. Support (explanatory): - "Compliance with the Standard in Paragraph 2 of this Section is achieved by having a method in place and using the method to maintain the minimum levels established in Table 2A-5. Provided that an assessment or management method is being used, an agency or official having jurisdiction would be in compliance with the Standard in Paragraph 2 of this Section even if there are some individual signs that do not meet the minimum retroreflectivity levels at a particular point in time." Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Table 2A-5, "Minimum Maintained Retroreflectivity Levels," including the White on Red row with minimum values of W ≥ 35 and R ≥ 7 (cd/lx/m² at 0.2° observation angle and −4.0° entrance angle) and the footnote requiring a minimum white-to-red contrast ratio of ≥ 3:1. Citations: - Section 2A.22 Standard §2 (p.62) - Section 2A.22 Support §3 (p.62) - Section 2A.22 Guidance §4 (p.62) - Section 2A.22 Option §5 (p.63) - Table 2A-5 (p.62)

**Gold answer:** Not automatically. The agency complies by having and using a method designed to maintain the minimums, even if some individual signs fall below them at a particular moment. For white on red, the table gives white at least 35, red at least 7 cd/lx/m², and at least a 3:1 white-to-red contrast ratio.

Except as provided in Paragraph 3 of this Section, where engineering judgment determines that sizes smaller than the prescribed dimensions are appropriate for use, the sign dimensions shall not be less than the minimum dimensions specified in this Manual. Guidance (recommended): - For guide sign legends, Table 2D-2 provides separate Single-Lane and Multi-Lane columns by speed range (Less than 30 mph, 30–40 mph, Greater than 40 mph); a 35-mph multi-lane roadway falls in the Multi-Lane 30–40 mph column, not the Single-Lane column. - For school-area regulatory signs, "The sizes in the Oversized column should be used on roadways that have four or more lanes with posted speed limits of 40 mph or higher" — a 35-mph multi-lane road does not trigger the Oversized recommendation, but the Conventional Road sizes still apply as the minimum. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Table 2D-2 (Sheet 1, Post-Mounted Signs) shows distinct Multi-Lane columns for the 30–40 mph range, confirming that multi-lane roads at 35 mph use larger legend sizes than the single-lane columns. - [Image 2] Table 7B-1 lists Conventional Road, Minimum, and Oversized sizes for school-area signs, and its notes state that minimum sign sizes for multi-lane conventional roads shall be as shown in the Conventional Road column. - [Image 5] Page 94 shows the Section 2A.07 Standard text restricting Minimum-column sizes to low-speed roadways with operating speeds of 30 mph or less. Citations: - Section 2A.07 Standard §2 (p.45) - Section 2D.07 Guidance §5 (p.210) - Section 7B.01 Guidance §3 (p.972) - Table 2D-2 (p.210) - Table 7B-1 (p.971) - Page 94 Note on insufficiency: The specific STOP sign (R1-1) size table for conventional roads is not included in the provided evidence, so the exact required STOP sign dimension (e.g., a specific width × height) cannot be quoted here. What can be established is that at 35 mph on a multi-lane road, no size smaller than the Conventional Road column value may be used.

**Gold answer:** For regulatory signs other than STOP, the single-lane size may be used at 35 mph or less. The STOP-sign exception remains: the multi-lane STOP size is 36 x 36 inches.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: For a potential stop condition (Condition B, deceleration to 0 mph — typical of Stop Ahead, Yield Ahead, and Signal Ahead signs), Table 2C-3 shows an advance placement distance of 495 feet for a 55-mph posted or 85th-percentile speed. This distance is not an inflexible mandate: Table 2C-3 is presented as guidelines that "should be applied with engineering judgment" and may be adjusted for roadway features, other signing, and visibility. However, in certain contexts (e.g., passive grade crossings with STOP/YIELD signs), a Standard does require locating the Stop/Yield Ahead sign "in accordance with Table 2C-3." Standards (mandatory): - "The Stop Ahead (W3-1), Yield Ahead (W3-2), and Signal Ahead (W3-3) Advance Traffic Control signs (see Figure 2C-8) shall be installed on an approach to a primary traffic control device that is not visible for a sufficient distance to permit the road user to respond to the device (see Table 2C-3)." - At passive grade crossings with a YIELD or STOP sign: "The Yield Ahead or Stop Ahead sign shall be located in accordance with Table 2C-3. The minimum distance between the signs shall be in accordance with Section 2C.04 and Table 2C-3." Guidance (recommended): - Table 2C-3 itself is a Guidance table: for a 55-mph speed, the potential stop condition (0-mph column) value is 495 feet; the Condition A value is 990 feet. - Per the table's footnote, "For Conditions A and B, warning signs with less than 6-inch legend or more than four words, a minimum of 100 feet should be added to the advance placement distance to provide adequate legibility of the warning sign." - "The effectiveness of the placement of warning signs should be periodically evaluated under both day and night conditions" — reflecting that the tabulated distances are guidelines to be applied with engineering judgment and can be adjusted for site conditions. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Table 2C-3 shows the 55-mph row with 495 ft in the 0-mph (potential stop) column of Condition B, and footnote 3 explains that the 0-mph condition corresponds to Stop Ahead, Yield Ahead, Signal Ahead, and Intersection Warning signs based on stopping sight distance with a 2.5-second PRT. [Image 2] The full page 152 shows Section 2C.04, where Guidance states the distances in Table 2C-3 "should be applied with engineering judgment," confirming the values are not inflexible. [Image 3] Figure 2C-13 (Sheet 5) illustrates a lane-merge sign sequence with the note "See Table 2C-3 to determine the advance placement distance," showing the table's use in practice. [Image 4] Figure 2C-13 (Sheet 1) similarly directs designers to Table 2C-3 for the W4-2R advance placement distance on a freeway lane-ends layout. [Image 5] Figure 2C-2 shows horizontal alignment sign placement with the note "See Table 2C-3 for advance placement distance guidelines," reinforcing the guideline nature of the table. Citations: - Table 2C-3 (p.152) - Section 2C.04 Guidance §804 (p.153) - Section 2C.04 Guidance §5 (p.152) - Section 2C.35 Standard §1 (p.177) - Section 8B.06 Standard §3 (p.1001)

**Gold answer:** The table shows 495 feet. It is a guideline to be applied with engineering judgment and may be adjusted for roadway features, other signing, and visibility; it is not an inflexible universal mandate.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: On a roadway with center line markings, a no-passing zone must be marked at a curve whenever the passing sight distance is less than the minimum in Table 3B-1 for the 85th-percentile speed or the speed limit (here, 55 mph). The zone itself must be marked with one-direction or two-direction no-passing zone markings. If the next no-passing zone starts less than 400 feet away, the recommended practice is to connect the two zones with continuous no-passing markings. Standards (mandatory): - "On roadways with center line markings, no-passing zone markings shall be used at horizontal or vertical curves where the passing sight distance is less than the minimum shown in Table 3B-1 for the 85th-percentile speed or the speed limit." - "On two-way, two-lane or three-lane roadways where center line markings are installed, no-passing zones shall be established at vertical and horizontal curves and other locations where an engineering study indicates that passing must be prohibited because of inadequate sight distances or other special conditions." - "No-passing zones shall be marked by either the one-direction no-passing zone pavement markings or the two-direction no-passing zone pavement markings described in Section 3B.01 and shown in Figures 3B-1 and 3B-3." Guidance (recommended): - Where the distance between successive no-passing zones is less than 400 feet, no-passing zone markings should connect the zones — so a gap of less than 400 feet after the curve's zone should be marked through as one continuous no-passing zone. Citations: - Section 3B.03 Standard §4 (p.543) - Section 3B.03 Standard §3 (p.543) - Section 3B.03 Standard §1 (p.542) - Section 3B.03 Guidance §7 (p.543) - Section 2B.36 Support §3 (p.102)

**Gold answer:** The no-passing zone must be marked when available passing sight distance is less than 900 feet. If successive no-passing zones are separated by less than 400 feet, the markings should connect them.

Guidance (recommended): - "The need for a traffic control signal should be considered if an engineering study finds that one of the following conditions exist for each of any 8 hours of an average day: A. The vehicles per hour given in both of the 100 percent columns of Condition A in Table 4C-1 exist on the major street and the more critical minor-street approach, respectively, to the intersection..." — this "should be considered" wording means warrant satisfaction does not mandate signal installation. - Alternatively, the warrant may be considered satisfied at reduced (80 percent) volume levels if both Condition A and Condition B are met at the 80 percent columns for each of any 8 hours of an average day. Options (permitted): - "Any four consecutive 15-minute periods may be considered as 1 hour if the separate 1-hour periods used in the warrant analysis do not overlap each other and both the major-street volume and the minor-street volume are for the same specific 1-hour periods." Meeting the warrant does not compel installation; an engineering study must determine whether a signal is justified.

In addition to the crash-history criterion, two other categories of conditions must also be met: (A) an adequate trial of alternatives with satisfactory observance and enforcement has failed to reduce the crash frequency, and (C) a traffic-volume condition applying to each of any 8 hours of an average day. Note that the specific numeric threshold values in Table 4C-2 and the full text of the volume criterion (C) are not included in the evidence provided. At least one of the following conditions applies to the reported crash history..." - For the one-year period specifically: "1. The number of reported angle crashes and pedestrian crashes within a 1-year period equals or exceeds the threshold number in Table 4C-2 for total angle crashes and pedestrian crashes (all severities); or 2. The number of reported fatal-and-injury angle crashes and pedestrian crashes within a 1-year period equals or exceeds the threshold number in Table 4C-2 for total fatal-and-injury angle crashes and pedestrian crashes." - The third criterion is a volume condition: "C. For each of any 8 hours of an average day, the ve..." (the remainder of this provision is truncated in the evidence provided). Options (permitted): - If the posted or statutory speed limit or the 85th-percentile speed on the major street exceeds 40 mph, or if the intersection lies within the built-up area of an isolated community of fewer than 10,000 population, the 56 percent volume columns in Table 4C-1 may be used in place of the 80 percent columns, and Tables 4C-4 and 4C-5 may be used in place of Tables 4C-2 and 4C-3 (Table 4C-4 provides the reduced one-year crash thresholds, with separate values for four-leg intersections). - Agencies may calibrate Highway Safety Manual (HSM) safety performance functions to their own crash data, or develop their own SPFs, and — when documented in the engineering study — may use agency-specific crash frequency values in place of the values in Tables 4C-2 through 4C-5. **Gold answer:** The thresholds are at least 5 all-severity angle/pedestrian crashes or at least 3 fatal-and-injury angle/pedestrian crashes. In addition, an adequate trial of alternatives must have failed, and the applicable 80-percent vehicular-volume or pedestrian-volume condition must be met for the required hours.

If approaching traffic does not have a continuous view of at least two signal faces for that distance, the recommended treatment is to install a warning sign (see Section 2C.35) to warn approaching traffic of the signal. Separately, at 45 mph or higher, signal backplates should be used on all signal faces facing the approach. (The table excerpt available here covers only 20–35 mph, so the 45-mph distance is not shown in the provided evidence.) - If approaching traffic does not have a continuous view of at least two signal faces for at least the minimum sight distance shown in Table 4D-2, a sign (see Section 2C.35) should be installed to warn approaching traffic of the traffic control signal. - If the posted or statutory speed limit or the 85th-percentile speed on an approach is 45 mph or higher, signal backplates should be used on all of the signal faces that face the approach. - On approaches with speeds of 45 mph or higher, for new or reconstructed installations: primary signal faces for through traffic should be provided per Table 4D-1; overhead faces should be located approximately over the center of each through lane when their number equals the number of through lanes; primary faces for mandatory turn lanes should be located overhead over the center of each such lane; all primary signal faces should be on the far side of the intersection; supplemental faces should be considered for visibility behind large vehicles; and all signal faces should have backplates. Citations: - Section 4D.06 Guidance §5 (p.670) - Section 4D.06 Guidance §6 (p.670) - Section 4D.06 Guidance §17 (p.671) - Section 4D.05 Guidance §7 (p.668)

**Gold answer:** They should be continuously visible from at least 460 feet in advance of the stop line. If at least two faces are not continuously visible for that distance, a warning sign should be installed for approaching traffic.

Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Per Figure 4I-4, the pedestrian intervals occur in this order: the Walk Interval (steady WALKING PERSON, 7 seconds minimum), then the Pedestrian Change Interval (flashing UPRAISED HAND, typically with a countdown display), then the Buffer Interval (steady UPRAISED HAND, 2 seconds minimum) before conflicting traffic is released. The buffer interval must always be provided and displayed, and the pedestrian clearance time is normally calculated using a walking speed of 3.5 feet per second, with slower speeds considered where slower pedestrians or wheelchair users routinely cross. Standards (mandatory): - "The Buffer Interval, which shall always be provided and displayed, may be used to help satisfy the calculated pedestrian clearance time, or may begin after the calculated pedestrian clearance time has ended." (Figure 4I-4 footnote) - As shown on the page with Figure 4I-3: "Following the pedestrian change interval, a buffer interval consisting of a steady UPRAISED HAND (symbolizing DONT WALK) signal indication shall be displayed for at least 2 seconds prior to the release of any conflicting vehicular movement. The sum of the time of the pedestrian change interval and the buffer interval shall not be less than the calculated pedestrian clearance time... The buffer interval shall not begin later than the beginning of the red clearance interval, if used." Guidance (recommended): - The pedestrian clearance time should be sufficient to allow a pedestrian who left the curb or edge of pavement at the end of the WALKING PERSON indication to travel at a walking speed of 3.5 feet per second to at least the far side of the traveled way or to a median of sufficient width for pedestrians to wait. - Where pedestrians who walk slower than 3.5 feet per second, or pedestrians who use wheelchairs, routinely use the crosswalk, a walking speed of less than 3.5 feet per second should be considered in determining the pedestrian clearance time. Options (permitted): - The additional time provided by an extended push button press to satisfy pedestrian clearance time needs may be added to either the walk interval or the pedestrian change interval. - The countdown display is optional for Pedestrian Change Intervals of 7 seconds or less (Figure 4I-4 footnote), and the Buffer Interval may be used to help satisfy the calculated pedestrian clearance time or may begin after the calculated pedestrian clearance time has ended. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 4I-4 shows the interval sequence — steady WALKING PERSON during the Walk Interval (7 seconds MIN.), flashing UPRAISED HAND with countdown during the Pedestrian Change Interval (spanning the calculated pedestrian clearance time), and steady UPRAISED HAND during the Buffer Interval (2 seconds MIN., with the "zero" point of the countdown at its start) — along with five possible relationships to the associated vehicular G/Y/R phase intervals. - [Image 2] The full page containing Figure 4I-4 also shows the Guidance that clearance time is based on a 3.5 feet per second walking speed and the Option that extended push button time may be added to the walk or pedestrian change interval. - [Image 3] This page's Standard paragraph 04 requires the pedestrian change interval (flashing UPRAISED HAND) to begin immediately after the WALKING PERSON indication, followed by a buffer interval of at least 2 seconds prior to release of any conflicting vehicular movement. - [Image 4] This page addresses leading pedestrian intervals and green intervals extending beyond the pedestrian clearance time, consistent with the last bar in Figure 4I-4. - [Image 5] This page concerns pedestrian hybrid beacon operation, noting flashing CIRCULAR RED may continue briefly after the pedestrian change interval to provide a buffer interval, paralleling the buffer concept in Figure 4I-4. Citations: - Section 4I.06 Support §6 (p.724) - Section 4I.06 Guidance §7 (p.724) - Section 4I.06 Option §9 (p.725) - Section 4I.06 Guidance §10 (p.725) - Figure 4I-4 (p.724) - Page 765 - Page 764 - Page 767 - Page 771

**Gold answer:** The order is WALK, flashing DONT WALK pedestrian change interval, then steady DONT WALK buffer interval. The buffer interval is at least 2 seconds before conflicting traffic is released, and clearance is normally evaluated using 3.5 feet per second.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Following pedestrian actuation, a pedestrian hybrid beacon proceeds through six stages: (1) dark until activated, (2) flashing yellow upon activation, (3) steady yellow, (4) both steady CIRCULAR RED indications, (5) alternating flashing CIRCULAR RED indications, and (6) dark again until the next actuation. The pedestrian walk interval corresponds to Stage 4, when both steady CIRCULAR RED signal indications are displayed to drivers; Stage 5 (alternating flashing red) corresponds to the pedestrian change interval. Standards (mandatory): - "Following an actuation by a pedestrian, a pedestrian hybrid beacon face shall display a flashing CIRCULAR yellow signal indication, followed by a steady CIRCULAR yellow signal indication, followed by both steady CIRCULAR RED signal indications during the pedestrian walk interval, followed by alternating flashing CIRCULAR RED signal indications during the pedestrian change interval (see Figure 4J-3). Upon … termination of the pedestrian change interval, the pedestrian hybrid beacon faces shall revert to a dark (not illuminated) condition." - "A pedestrian hybrid beacon face shall consist of three signal sections, with a CIRCULAR YELLOW signal indication centered below two horizontally-aligned CIRCULAR RED signal indications (see Figure 4J-3)." - "Except as otherwise provided in this Section, a pedestrian hybrid beacon shall meet the provisions of Chapters 4D through 4G, 4I, and 4J." Options (permitted): - Per the note in Figure 4J-3, "An optional steady red clearance interval may be added after Interval 3, and an optional short buffer interval (alternating flashing red while the pedestrian signal heads are displaying a steady UPRAISED HAND) may be added after Interval 5." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 4J-3 depicts the six-interval sequence — 1. Dark Until Activated; 2. Flashing Yellow Upon Activation; 3. Steady Yellow; 4. Steady Red During Pedestrian Walk Interval; 5. Alternating Flashing Red During Pedestrian Change Interval; 6. Dark Again Until Activated — with a legend (SY steady yellow, FY flashing yellow, SR steady red, FR flashing red) explicitly labeling Interval 4 as the walk interval. Citations: - Section 4J.03 Standard §2 (p.730) - Section 4J.02 Standard §2 (p.728) - Section 4J.02 Standard §1 (p.728) - Figure 4J-3 (p.729)

**Gold answer:** Dark, flashing yellow, steady yellow, both steady red, alternating flashing red, then dark. The both-steady-red stage corresponds to the pedestrian walk interval.

The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 5A.03, Section 1C.03, Section 5A.02, Table 5A-1, p.760, p.31. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Level 2 performs steering and acceleration/deceleration but still expects the human to perform all remaining aspects of the task, so it is ADAS rather than ADS. | Level 3 performs all aspects within its ODD, subject to a human fallback request. | An ADS performs the entire dynamic driving task on a sustained basis and the term applies to Levels 3-5. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The MUTCD defines an Automated Driving System (ADS) as hardware and software capable of performing the *entire* Dynamic Driving Task (DDT) on a sustained basis, a term applied specifically to Levels 3, 4, and 5. Level 2 does not qualify because, per Table 5A-1, it executes only steering and acceleration/deceleration while "the human driver performs all remaining aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task" — so the system performs only part of the DDT and falls in the ADAS category. Level 3, by contrast, is "sustained performance by an ADS of all aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task within a given ODD," satisfying the full-DDT criterion (the human is only expected to respond to a request to intervene), so it is classed as ADS. Standards (mandatory): - The acronym is formally established: "ADS-Automated Driving System" (among the acronyms that "shall have the following meanings"). Support (informational): - "Automated Driving System (ADS) - The hardware and software that are collectively capable of performing the entire Dynamic Driving Task (DDT) on a sustained basis, regardless of whether it is limited to a specific Operational Design Domain (ODD); this term is used specifically to describe a Level 3, 4, or 5 driving automation system." - The broader term "Driving Automation System" covers systems performing "part or all of the DDT on a sustained basis" (Levels 1–5), which is why Level 2 is still a driving automation system but not an ADS. - Table 5A-1 assigns Levels 1–2 to the "Advanced Driver Assistance Systems (ADAS)" category and Levels 3–5 to the "Automated Driving System (ADS)" category; Level 2's description expressly leaves remaining DDT aspects to the human driver, while Level 3's description covers "all aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task within a given ODD." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Table 5A-1 shows the "Automation Category" column grouping Levels 1–2 under ADAS and Levels 3–5 under ADS, with Level 2's description requiring the human driver to perform all remaining DDT aspects and Level 3's covering all aspects of the DDT. [Image 2] The full page shows both the definitions list (including the ADS definition in Item B) and Table 5A-1 confirming the Level 2/ADAS vs. Level 3/ADS split. [Image 4] The page presents Part 5's general overview and the definitions section (5A.03) that anchors the ADS/ADAS distinction. Citations: - Section 5A.03 Support §1 (p.760) - Table 5A-1 (p.760) - Section 1C.03 Standard §1 (p.31) - Section 5A.02 Support §1 (p.759)

**Gold answer:** An ADS performs the entire dynamic driving task on a sustained basis and the term applies to Levels 3-5. Level 2 performs steering and acceleration/deceleration but still expects the human to perform all remaining aspects of the task, so it is ADAS rather than ADS. Level 3 performs all aspects within its ODD, subject to a human fallback request.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: In the direction of travel, a TTC zone consists of four areas: the advance warning area (tells traffic what to expect ahead), the transition area (moves traffic out of its normal path), the activity area (where work takes place), and the termination area (lets traffic resume normal operations). The activity area itself is comprised of three components: the work space, the traffic space, and the buffer space. All of this material is Support (informational), not a mandatory requirement. Guidance (recommended): - No Guidance provisions on this point appear in the evidence; the descriptions of the four areas and the three activity-area components are Support statements only. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 6B-1 depicts the four areas in travel order—Advance Warning Area, Transition Area (with shoulder taper), Activity Area, and Termination Area (with downstream taper)—and labels the activity area's work space ("set aside for workers, equipment, and material storage"), traffic space ("allows traffic to pass through the activity area"), and lateral and longitudinal buffer spaces ("provides protection for traffic and workers"). - [Image 2] Page 814 contains Section 6B.06, which states the activity area "is comprised of the work space, the traffic space, and the buffer space." - [Image 3] Page 812 contains Sections 6B.03–6B.05, which enumerate the four areas (advance warning, transition, activity, termination) and describe the advance warning and transition areas. - [Image 4] Page 817 discusses taper types (shifting, shoulder, downstream) associated with the transition and termination areas shown in the figure. - [Image 5] Page 822 addresses worker safety considerations related to activities within the work space and activity area. Support (informational): - "A TTC zone is often divided into four areas as needed, based on engineering judgment: the advance warning area, the transition area, the activity area, and the termination area. Figure 6B-1 illustrates the four areas typically included in a TTC zone." - "The activity area is the section of the highway where the work activity takes place. It is comprised of the work space, the traffic space, and the buffer space." - "The traffic space is the portion of the highway in which road users are routed through the activity area." Citations: - Section 6B.03 Support §1 (p.771) - Section 6B.06 Support §1 (p.773) - Section 6B.06 Support §5 (p.773) - Figure 6B-1 (p.772) - Page 814 - Page 812

**Gold answer:** Advance warning, transition, activity, and termination. The activity area consists of the work space, traffic space, and buffer space.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: For a speed of 35 mph (40 mph or less), Table 6B-4 gives L = WS²/60 = (12 × 35²)/60 = (12 × 1,225)/60 = **245 feet**. Applying Table 6B-3: the merging taper must be at least L = **245 ft**, the shifting taper at least 0.5 L = **≈123 ft**, and the shoulder taper at least 0.33 L = **≈81 ft**. Guidance (recommended): - "The appropriate taper length (L) should be determined using the criteria shown in Tables 6B-3 and 6B-4." - "A shifting taper should have a length of approximately ½ L (see Tables 6B-3 and 6B-4)." - "If used, shoulder tapers should have a length of approximately ¹⁄3 L (see Tables 6B-3 and 6B-4). If a shoulder is used as a travel lane, either through practice or during a TTC activity, a normal merging or shifting taper should be used." - The same formula convention appears in the markings chapter: "For roadways where the speed limit is less than 45 mph, the formula L = WS2/60 should be used to compute the taper length," where W is the offset width in feet and S the speed in mph. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Table 6B-4 shows that for speeds of 40 mph or less, L = WS²/60, with L = taper length in feet, W = width of offset in feet, and S = posted speed limit, off-peak 85th-percentile speed, or anticipated operating speed in mph. - [Image 2] Table 6B-3 lists minimum taper lengths: Merging Taper "at least L," Shifting Taper "at least 0.5 L," Shoulder Taper "at least 0.33 L," and notes "Use Table 6B-4 to calculate L." - [Image 3] Figure 6B-2 illustrates the merging taper (L), shifting tapers (1/2 L), and shoulder taper (1/3 L) in a typical TTC zone layout. - [Image 4] Figure 6P-39 (TA-39) shows taper lengths L and 1/2 L applied in a median crossover on a freeway, referring to Table 6B-4 for calculating L. - [Image 5] Figure 6P-38 (TA-38) shows an interior lane closure using L and 1/2 L taper dimensions with a shoulder taper, also referencing Table 6B-4. - [Image 6] Figure 6P-42 (TA-42) shows L-length tapers and shoulder tapers in the vicinity of an exit ramp, again referencing Table 6B-4 for L. Citations: - Table 6B-4 (p.775) - Table 6B-3 (p.775) - Section 6B.08 Guidance §4 (p.775) - Section 6B.08 Guidance §8 (p.776) - Section 6B.08 Guidance §10 (p.776) - Section 6B.08 Support §2 (p.775) - Section 3B.13 Guidance §2 (p.568) - Page 815

**Gold answer:** For 40 mph or less, L = W*S²/60 = 12*35²/60 = 245 feet. Minimum merging taper: 245 feet; shifting taper: 122.5 feet; shoulder taper: about 80.9 feet (0.33L, commonly rounded appropriately in design).

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The STOP/SLOW paddle is the primary and preferred hand-signaling device because it gives road users more positive guidance than red flags; flags are limited to emergency situations per Figure 6D-1. To stop traffic, the flagger must face road users and aim the STOP paddle face toward them in a stationary position with the arm extended horizontally, while holding the free arm with the palm above shoulder level toward approaching traffic. Standards (mandatory): - "To stop road users, the flagger shall face road users and aim the STOP paddle face toward road users in a stationary position with the arm extended horizontally away from the body. The free arm shall be held with the palm of the hand above shoulder level toward approaching traffic." Guidance (recommended): - "The STOP/SLOW paddle (see Figure 6D-1 and Table 6G-1) should be the primary and preferred hand-signaling device because the STOP/SLOW paddle gives road users more positive guidance than red flags." Support (informational): - The optimum display method is to place the STOP/SLOW paddle on a rigid staff tall enough that, when the end of the staff rests on the ground, the message is high enough to be seen by approaching or stopped traffic. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 6D-1 labels the STOP/SLOW paddle as the "PREFERRED METHOD" (with the flag reserved for "EMERGENCY SITUATIONS ONLY") and depicts the stop-traffic posture — flagger holding the R1-1 STOP paddle (18 inches minimum) on a staff with the free arm raised, palm toward traffic. Citations: - Section 6D.02 Guidance §1 (p.783) - Section 6D.05 Standard §3 (p.785) - Section 6D.02 Support §5 (p.783) - Figure 6D-1 (p.784)

**Gold answer:** The STOP/SLOW paddle is the primary and preferred device. To stop traffic, the flagger faces traffic, displays the STOP face in a stationary position, and raises the free arm with the palm toward traffic.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Per Figure 6L-3, a Type C arrow board has a minimum size of 96 x 48 inches, a minimum legibility distance of 1 mile, and a minimum of 15 elements. It must also provide three categories of display modes: (A) a Flashing Arrow, Sequential Arrow, or Sequential Chevron mode; (B) a flashing Double Arrow mode; and (C) a flashing Caution or Alternating Diamond mode. Type C boards are intended for high-speed, high-volume traffic control projects. Standards (mandatory): - "Arrow boards shall meet the minimum size, legibility distance, number of elements, and other specifications shown in Figure 6L-3." For Type C, Figure 6L-3 specifies 96 x 48 inches minimum size, 1 mile minimum legibility distance, and 15 minimum elements. - "An arrow board shall have the following three mode selections: A. A Flashing Arrow, Sequential Arrow, or Sequential Chevron mode; B. A flashing Double Arrow mode; and C. A flashing Caution or Alternating Diamond mode." - "The arrow board shall have suitable elements capable of the various operating modes. The color presented by the elements shall be yellow." - "An arrow board shall be a sign with a matrix of elements capable of either flashing or sequential displays." Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Figure 6L-3, whose table lists Type C with a minimum size of 96 x 48 inches, minimum legibility distance of 1 mile, and 15 minimum elements, and illustrates the required operating modes (Flashing Arrow/Sequential Arrow/Sequential Chevron; Flashing Double Arrow; Flashing Caution or Alternating Diamond Caution) using a Type C board. [Image 2] presents the same Figure 6L-3 page (p.834) in full-page view confirming the Type C row and mode illustrations. [Image 3] shows the Section 6L.06 text page (p.833) containing the Standard provisions on the three required mode selections and conformance with Figure 6L-3. Citations: - Section 6L.06 Standard §5 (p.833) - Section 6L.06 Standard §16 (p.833) - Section 6L.06 Standard §13 (p.833) - Section 6L.06 Standard §1 (p.832) - Section 6L.06 Support §6 (p.833) - Figure 6L-3 (p.834)

**Gold answer:** Minimum size 96 x 48 inches, minimum legibility distance 1 mile, and at least 15 elements. It must provide at least one directional merge mode, the flashing double-arrow mode, and at least one caution mode.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Entering a reduced school speed limit zone, drivers must first encounter a School (S1-1) sign in advance, then the School Speed Limit assembly or School Speed Limit When Flashing (S5-1) sign placed at or near the point where the zone begins; a Reduced School Speed Limit Ahead (S4-5/S4-5a) sign is recommended in advance where the speed reduction exceeds 10 mph, and if used it must be followed by the School Speed Limit sign or assembly. Leaving the zone, the downstream end must be marked with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign, with the resumed speed limit (R2-1) optionally shown. Figure 7B-2 illustrates this full sequence in both travel directions. Standards (mandatory): - "A School Speed Limit assembly (see Figure 7B-1) or a School Speed Limit When Flashing (S5-1) sign (see Figure 7B-1) shall be used to indicate the speed limit where a reduced school speed limit zone has been established based upon an engineering study or where a reduced school speed limit is specified for such areas by statute. The School Speed Limit assembly or School Speed Limit When Flashing sign shall be placed at or as near as practicable to the point where the reduced school speed limit zone begins (see Figures 7B-2 and 7B-4)." - "If a reduced school speed limit zone has been established, a School (S1-1) sign shall be installed in advance (see Table 2C-3 for advance placement guidelines) of the first School Speed Limit sign assembly or S5-1 sign that is encountered in each direction as traffic approaches the reduced school speed limit zone (see Figures 7B-2 and 7B-4)." - "If used, the Reduced School Speed Limit Ahead sign shall be followed by a School Speed Limit sign or a School Speed Limit assembly." - "Except as provided in Paragraph 4 of this Section, the downstream end of an authorized and posted reduced school speed limit zone shall be identified with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign (see Figures 7B-1, 7B-2, and 7B-4)." - Where higher fines apply only to speeding: "a FINES HIGHER (R2-6P), FINES DOUBLE (R2-6aP), or $XX FINE (R2-6bP) plaque shall be posted with the School Speed Limit (S5-1) sign and shall not be posted beneath the School Zone (S1-1) sign (see Section 7B.05)." Guidance (recommended): - "A Reduced School Speed Limit Ahead (S4-5 or S4-5a) sign (see Figure 7B-1) should be used to inform road users of a reduced speed zone where the speed limit is being reduced by more than 10 mph, or where engineering judgment indicates that advance notice would be appropriate." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 7B-2 shows the complete two-direction sequence: an advance School (S1-1) sign with optional SCHOOL (S4-3P) plaque, a Reduced School Speed Limit Ahead (S4-5) sign, the School Speed Limit assembly (S4-3P/R2-1/S4-1P) at the zone beginning, and at the downstream end an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) or END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) sign with an optional Speed Limit 45 (R2-1) sign above. - [Image 2] Figure 7B-1 depicts the component signs of the sequence, including the School Speed Limit assembly options (S4-3P, R2-1, S4-1P, S4-2P, S4-4P, S4-6P), the S5-1 School Speed Limit When Flashing sign, the S4-5/S4-5a Reduced School Speed Limit Ahead signs, and the S5-2/S5-3 end-of-zone signs. - [Image 3] The Section 7B.05 text page shows the Standard paragraphs establishing the S1-1 advance sign, zone-beginning assembly placement, and S5-3 end-of-zone requirements. - [Image 4] The Section 7B.06/7B.07 page contains the higher-fines plaque provisions and the option for using an END SCHOOL ZONE sign where zones end coincidentally. - [Image 5] Full-page view of Figure 7B-2 confirming the layout of signs approaching and leaving the school speed zone in both directions. - [Image 6] Figure 7B-4 shows an analogous sequence for a school zone with higher fines only for speeding, with fines plaques (R2-6P/R2-6aP/R2-6bP) posted below the School Speed Limit assembly. Citations: - Section 7B.05 Standard §1 (p.978) - Section 7B.05 Standard §2 (p.978) - Section 7B.05 Standard §14 (p.980) - Section 7B.05 Standard §3 (p.978) - Section 7B.06 Standard §3 (p.980) - Section 7B.05 Guidance §13 (p.980) - Figure 7B-2 (p.975) - Figure 7B-1 (p.973) - Page 1019 - Page 1024 - Page 1016 - Page 1020

**Gold answer:** Approaching traffic first encounters an advance School (S1-1) sign, then the School Speed Limit assembly or S5-1 at the beginning of the reduced zone. The downstream end is normally marked by S5-3 END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT; S5-2 END SCHOOL ZONE may be used when both zones end together, with a downstream R2-1 optionally mounted.
