Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The Crossbuck is at the top, the inverted-T Number of Tracks plaque is directly below it, and the YIELD or STOP sign is below the plaque on the support. | The Number of Tracks plaque is required when two or more tracks are present. **Model answer:** Direct Answer: Per Figure 8B-2, the assembly is arranged from top to bottom as: the Crossbuck (R15-1) sign at the top (mounted at approximately 9 feet, which may be varied or increased to accommodate signs below it), then the inverted-T Number of Tracks (R15-2P) plaque mounted directly below the Crossbuck, and finally the YIELD (R1-2) or STOP (R1-1) sign below the plaque on the same support. The Number of Tracks plaque is required whenever two or more tracks are present at the grade crossing. Standards (mandatory): - "If there are two or more tracks at a grade crossing, the number of tracks shall be indicated on a supplemental Number of Tracks (R15-2P) plaque (see Figure 8B-1) of inverted T shape mounted below the Crossbuck sign in the manner shown in Figure 8B-2." - "A Crossbuck Assembly shall consist of a Crossbuck (R15-1) sign, and a Number of Tracks (R15-2P) plaque if two or more tracks are present, that complies with the provisions of Section 8B.03, and either a YIELD (R1-2) or STOP (R1-1) sign installed on the same support, except as provided in Paragraph 10 of this Section. YIELD or STOP signs used at passive grade crossings shall be installed in compliance with the provisions of Section 2B.18, and Figures 8B-2 and 8B-3." - "If a Crossbuck sign support at a passive grade crossing does not include a YIELD or STOP sign (either because the YIELD or STOP sign is placed on a separate support or because a YIELD or STOP sign is not present on the approach), a vertical strip of retroreflective white material, not less than 2 inches in width, shall be used for the full length of the front of the support from the Crossbuck sign or Number of Tracks plaque to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway." - Per the figure notes accompanying Figure 8B-2: mounting height "shall be at least 4 feet for installations of YIELD or STOP signs on existing Crossbuck sign supports" and "shall be at least 5 feet for new installations in rural areas and at least 7 feet for new installations in areas where parking or pedestrian movements are likely." Guidance (recommended): - The use of STOP signs at passive grade crossings should be limited to unusual conditions where a Diagnostic Team determines that requiring all motor vehicles to make a full stop is necessary, considering line of sight, number of tracks, train and vehicle speeds, and crash history. - If the YIELD or STOP sign is instead installed on a separate support (see Figure 8B-3), it should be placed in the same plane as the Crossbuck sign and closer to the traveled way, with a minimum 2-inch separation between the nearest points of the two signs. Options (permitted): - A strip of retroreflective white material not less than 2 inches in width may be used on the back of each blade of each Crossbuck sign for the length of each blade at active grade crossings. - Per Figure 8B-2 notes, the 9-foot Crossbuck mounting height may be varied as required by local conditions and may be increased to accommodate signs mounted below the Crossbuck sign. Visual evidence: [Image 1] (Figure 8B-2) shows the vertical order on a single support—Crossbuck at 9 ft (varied as needed), the inverted-T "3 TRACKS" plaque immediately below, then the YIELD or STOP sign below that, with retroreflective strips on the support extending to within 2 ft of the roadway edge. [Image 2] (Figure 8B-3) shows the alternative arrangement with the YIELD sign on a separate support placed closer to the traveled way with a 2-inch minimum separation from the Crossbuck. [Image 3] (Figure 8E-5) shows the same vertical stacking (Crossbuck, "2 TRACKS" plaque, YIELD or STOP) applied at a pathway or sidewalk grade crossing. Citations: - Section 8B.03 Standard §6 (p.993) - Section 8B.04 Standard §1 (p.995) - Section 8B.04 Standard §20 (p.1000) - Section 8B.04 Guidance §6 (p.997) - Section 8B.04 Guidance §13 (p.1000) - Section 8B.03 Option §9 (p.995) - Figure 8B-2 (p.993) - Figure 8B-3 (p.998) - Figure 8E-5 (p.1039)

**Gold answer:** The Crossbuck is at the top, the inverted-T Number of Tracks plaque is directly below it, and the YIELD or STOP sign is below the plaque on the support. The Number of Tracks plaque is required when two or more tracks are present.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A bicycle box must be at least 10 feet long (measured from the upstream edge nearest the motor-vehicle stop line to the downstream edge nearest the crosswalk/intersection) and must contain at least one bicycle symbol marking. Turns on red are prohibited from any lane where a bicycle box is placed. Green-colored pavement is optional in a bicycle box, but if it is used, it must cover the full limits of the box. Standards (mandatory): - "If used, the distance from the upstream edge of the bicycle box that is nearest to the stop line for motor vehicles to the downstream edge of the bicycle box that is nearest the crosswalk or intersection shall be at least 10 feet. At least one bicycle symbol marking (see Figure 9E-12) shall be used in the bicycle box." - "Turns on red shall be prohibited from the lane where a bicycle box is placed." (Section 9E.12 Standard, shown on the page view in [Image 3]) - "If used, green-colored pavement shall be used in the full limits of the bicycle box." (Section 9E.12 Standard, shown with Figure 9E-12 Sheet 1 in [Image 1]/[Image 4]) - "If used, green-colored pavement shall be limited to: ... E. Bicycle Boxes (see Section 9E.12) ..." (i.e., bicycle boxes are one of the only permitted applications of green pavement). Options (permitted): - A bicycle box may be used to increase the visibility of stopped bicycles on the approach to a signalized intersection during the red signal indication for the approach lane(s) behind the box. - "Green-colored pavement may be used in a bicycle box." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 9E-12 (Sheet 1) shows a bicycle box across one lane with the "10 ft MIN." dimension between the advance stop line and the intersection stop line, bicycle symbol markings inside the box, and optional green-colored pavement filling the box. - [Image 2] Figure 9E-12 (Sheet 2, p.1107) shows a bicycle box across multiple lanes with the "10 ft MIN." dimension, green pavement over the full box, and the NO TURN ON RED (R10-11) sign posted, illustrating the turn-on-red prohibition. - [Image 3] The Section 9E.12 page view shows the Standard paragraphs including the 10-foot minimum/bicycle symbol requirement (§06) and "Turns on red shall be prohibited from the lane where a bicycle box is placed" (§09). - [Image 4] The Sheet 1 page view repeats the Standard "If used, green-colored pavement shall be used in the full limits of the bicycle box." - [Image 5] Section 3H.06 confirms that green-colored pavement, if used, shall be limited to specified bicycle facilities including Bicycle Boxes (item E). **Gold answer:** The bicycle box must be at least 10 feet long, include at least one bicycle symbol, prohibit turns on red from its lane, and, if green pavement is used, fill the entire limits of the box.

## T001

**Question:** What is the purpose of the MUTCD, and what four objectives are used to achieve that purpose?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1A.01, Section 1B.01, p.1, p.4, 23 U.S.C. 109(d). Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The MUTCD establishes uniform national criteria for traffic control devices on streets, highways, pedestrian and bicycle facilities, and site roadways open to public travel. | Its objectives are to promote safety, inclusion, and mobility | promote consistency in use, installation, and operation Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.832 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The purpose of the MUTCD is to establish uniform national criteria for the use of traffic control devices that meet the needs and expectancy of road users on all streets, highways, pedestrian and bicycle facilities, and site roadways open to public travel. This purpose is achieved through four objectives: (A) promoting safety, inclusion, and mobility for all road users; (B) promoting efficiency through national uniformity in the meaning and appearance of traffic control devices; (C) promoting national consistency in the use, installation, and operation of traffic control devices; and (D) providing basic principles for traffic engineers to use in decisions about the use, installation, operation, maintenance, and removal of traffic control devices. Note that these statements are Support (explanatory) provisions, not normative requirements; the MUTCD's mandatory legal status is established separately, in that it "shall be recognized as the national standard for all traffic control devices installed on any street, highway, bikeway, or site roadway open to public travel." Standards (mandatory): - "The Manual on Uniform Traffic Control Devices for Streets and Highways (MUTCD) is incorporated by reference in 23 Code of Federal Regulations (CFR), Part 655, Subpart F and shall be recognized as the national standard for all traffic control devices installed on any street, highway, bikeway, or site roadway open to public travel... in accordance with 23 U.S.C. 109(d) and 402(a)." Citations: - Section 1A.01 Support §1 (p.1) - Section 1A.01 Support §2 (p.1) - Section 1B.01 Standard §1 (p.4)

**Gold answer:** The MUTCD establishes uniform national criteria for traffic control devices on streets, highways, pedestrian and bicycle facilities, and site roadways open to public travel. Its objectives are to promote safety, inclusion, and mobility; promote efficiency through national uniformity in device meaning and appearance; promote consistency in use, installation, and operation; and provide basic decision-making principles for traffic engineers.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. The MUTCD's applicability to facilities open to public travel does not depend on whether the facility is publicly or privately owned, nor on the source of its funding. What matters is whether the facility is open to public travel — the MUTCD explicitly applies to both publicly and privately owned streets, roadways, bikeways, and toll roads open to the public, and does not apply to certain facilities (such as fully gated private roadways and parking areas) regardless of ownership. Standards (mandatory): - "In accordance with 23 CFR 655.603(a), the MUTCD shall apply to all of the following types of facilities: A. Any street, roadway, or bikeway open to public travel, either publicly or privately owned; B. Streets and roadways on sites that are off the public right-of-way that are open to public travel without full-time access restrictions... C. Publicly-owned toll roads, including those under the jurisdiction of a public agency, public authority, or public-private partnership; D. Privately-owned toll roads where the public is allowed to travel without access restriction... and E. Grade crossings of publicly-owned roadways with railroads or light rail transit." - "The MUTCD shall not apply to the following types of facilities: A. Roadways within private gated properties where access to the general public is restricted at all times; B. Grade crossings of privately-owned roadways with railroads; and C. Parking areas, including the driving aisles within those parking areas, that are either publicly or privately owned." Citations: - Section 1A.01 Support §5 (p.1) - Section 1B.01 Standard §2 (p.4) - Section 1B.01 Standard §3 (p.4)

**Gold answer:** No. Applicability is independent of public or private ownership or jurisdiction and independent of whether funding is Federal, State, local, or private.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. The MUTCD explicitly explains (as Support) that infrastructure elements restricting travel paths or vehicle speeds—such as islands, curbs, speed humps, and other raised roadway surfaces—are not traffic control devices, and that transverse or longitudinal rumble strips are also not traffic control devices. This is consistent with the mandatory definition of traffic control devices, which covers signs, signals, markings, channelizing devices, or other devices whose primary purpose is communicating a regulatory, warning, or guidance message. Standards (mandatory): - "Traffic control devices shall be defined as all signs, signals, markings, channelizing devices, or other devices that use colors, shapes, symbols, words, sounds, or tactile information for the primary purpose of communicating a regulatory, warning, or guidance message to road users on a street, highway, pedestrian facility, bikeway, pathway, or site roadway open to public travel." Physical infrastructure elements like islands, curbs, speed humps, and rumble strips do not fit this definition because their primary function is restricting travel paths or speeds, not communicating a message. Visual evidence: (none) Citations: - Section 1A.02 Support §2 (p.1) - Section 3I.01 Support §2 (p.632) - Section 6A.03 Standard §2 (p.768) Supporting notes (informational, non-normative): Section 1A.02 states that islands, curbs, speed humps, other raised roadway surfaces, and rumble strips are not traffic control devices, and that operational devices such as fencing, roadway lighting, barriers, and attenuators are shown in the Manual only for context. Section 3I.01 confirms that raised islands, "[a]lthough they are not considered to be traffic control devices," are sometimes used to channelize traffic.

**Gold answer:** No. Infrastructure elements that physically restrict travel paths or speeds, including islands, curbs, speed humps, other raised surfaces, and transverse or longitudinal rumble strips, are not traffic control devices.

Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: A Standard is mandatory or prohibitive and typically uses 'shall.' Guidance is recommended practice with justified deviations allowed and typically uses 'should.' An Option is per… Adjudication note: Correct Standard, Guidance, and Option distinctions; the full Support definition is not stated. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Section 1C.01 defines the four headings: a Standard is a mandatory or prohibitive requirement (typically using "shall"), Guidance is a recommended practice with deviations allowed based on engineering judgment or study (typically using "should"), and an Option is a permissive condition carrying no requirement or recommendation, sometimes modifying a Standard or Guidance statement. The evidence provided is truncated before the full definition of Support, but other sections show it is used for explanatory/informational material (e.g., notes and typical application content clearly classified under these headings). Standards (mandatory): - "When used in this Manual, the text headings of Standard, Guidance, Option, and Support shall be defined as follows: A. Standard—a statement of required, mandatory, or specifically prohibitive practice regarding a traffic control device... All Standard statements are labeled, and the text appears in bold type. The verb 'shall' is typically used. The verbs 'should' and 'may' are not used in Standard statements. Standard statements are sometimes modified by Option statements." - "B. Guidance—a statement of recommended practice in typical situations, with deviations allowed if engineering judgment or engineering study (see Section 1D.03) indicates the deviation to be appropriate. All Guidance statements are labeled, and the text appears in unbold italic type. The verb 'should' is typically used. The verbs 'shall' and 'may' are not used in Guidance statements. Guidance statements are sometimes modified by Option statements." - "C. Option—a statement of practice that is a permissive condition and carries no requirement or recommendation. Option statements sometimes contain allowable modifications to a Standard or Guidance statement." - "In limited, location-specific cases, the results of a documented engineering study (see Section 1D.03) might indicate a deviation from one or more requirements of a Standard provision to be appropriate." Support (explanatory, from evidence): - The supplied text of Section 1C.01 is cut off before the complete definition of Support, so its full verbatim definition (including its typical verb usage) cannot be quoted from this evidence. - Section 6P.01 illustrates how the headings function in practice: "Except for the notes (which are clearly classified using headings as being Standard, Guidance, Option, or Support), the information presented in the typical applications can generally be regarded as Guidance." Citations: - Section 1C.01 Standard §1 (p.13) - Section 6P.01 Support §2 (p.857)

**Gold answer:** A Standard is mandatory or prohibitive and typically uses 'shall.' Guidance is recommended practice with justified deviations allowed and typically uses 'should.' An Option is permissive and typically uses 'may.' Support is informational and conveys no mandate, recommendation, permission, prohibition, or enforceable condition.

Deviations from a Standard provision are allowed only when the agency "fully documents, through an engineering study, the engineering basis for the deviation," whereas deviations from Guidance may rest on either engineering judgment or an engineering study. Note that the actual definitions of "engineering study" and "engineering judgment" are located in Section 1C.02, which is not included in this evidence, so the precise definitional wording cannot be quoted here. Standards (mandatory): - "Where the content of this Manual requires a decision for implementation, such decisions shall be made by an engineer, or an individual under the supervision of an engineer, who has the appropriate levels of experience and expertise to make the traffic control device decision. Those decisions shall be made using engineering judgment or engineering study, as required by the MUTCD provision." - For Standard statements: "In limited, location-specific cases, the results of a documented engineering study (see Section 1D.03) might indicate a deviation from one or more requirements of a Standard provision to be appropriate." - For Guidance statements: deviations are "allowed if engineering judgment or engineering study (see Section 1D.03) indicates the deviation to be appropriate" — i.e., either mechanism suffices for Guidance, but only a documented study supports deviation from a Standard. Citations: - Section 1A.04 Standard §2 (p.2) - Section 1C.01 Standard §1 (p.13) - Section 1D.03 Support §1 (p.32) - Section 1D.03 Support §2 (p.32)

**Gold answer:** Both involve evaluating pertinent information and applying engineering principles by a professional engineer or a person under such an engineer's supervision. Engineering judgment does not require documentation; an engineering study must be documented in writing.

The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Yes — roadways within shopping centers that are open to public travel without full-time access restrictions are "site roadways open to public travel," and the MUTCD applies to them regardless of whether they are publicly or privately owned. Streets and roadways on sites that are off the public right-of-way that are open to public travel without full-time access restrictions. Examples include roadways within shopping centers, office parks, airports, sports arenas, other similar business and/or recreation facilities... The above-described examples of streets and roadways are referred to in this Manual as site roadways open to public travel." - "The MUTCD shall not apply to the following types of facilities: ... C. Parking areas, including the driving aisles within those parking areas, that are either publicly or privately owned." Guidance (recommended): - Before requesting permission to experiment with a new device or application, an owner of a site roadway open to public travel should first check for any laws, regulations, and/or directives covering the application of the MUTCD that might apply. Citations: - Section 1B.01 Standard §2 (p.4) - Section 1B.01 Standard §3 (p.4) - Section 1A.01 Support §5 (p.1) - Section 1B.05 Guidance §7 (p.7)

**Gold answer:** It applies to the shopping-center site roadways that are open to public travel without full-time access restrictions. It does not apply to the parking area itself, including its driving aisles.

## T009

**Question:** When a non-compliant traffic control device is replaced because it is damaged, missing, or no longer serviceable, what is the general rule and the limited replacement-in-kind exception?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1C.02, Section 1B.03, Section 5A.04, Section 6K.01, Section 1D.10, p.6. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: A non-compliant device may be replaced in kind only when engineering judgment finds that a lone compliant device among adjacent non-compliant devices would confuse users and/or th… | The replacement must generally be a compliant device. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.500. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The general rule is that a non-compliant device being replaced or refurbished because it is damaged, missing, or no longer serviceable must be replaced with a compliant device. The limited exception permits replacement in kind, based on engineering judgment, when a single compliant device within a series of adjacent non-compliant devices would confuse road users and/or the replacement schedule for the whole series will still achieve timely MUTCD compliance. Standards (mandatory): - "A non-compliant traffic control device that is being replaced or refurbished because it is damaged, missing, or no longer serviceable (see definition in Section 1C.02) for any reason shall be replaced with a compliant device, except as provided for in Paragraph 13 of this Section." - "Unless a particular device is no longer serviceable (see definition in Section 1C.02), non-compliant devices on existing highways and bikeways shall be brought into compliance with the current edition of the National MUTCD as part of the systematic upgrading of substandard traffic control devices (and installation of new required traffic control devices) required pursuant to the Highway Safety Program, 23 U.S.C. §402(a)." - "Channelizing devices that are no longer serviceable (see definition in Section 1C.02) shall be replaced." Guidance (recommended): - Agencies should establish maintenance policies that incorporate effective practices to identify and then fix or replace in a timely manner any traffic control device that is reaching the end of its useful life, or that is damaged or otherwise no longer serviceable, along with applying uniform devices, planning compliant temporary/emergency traffic control, and removing extraneous devices. - Functional maintenance of traffic control devices should be used to determine if certain devices need to be changed to meet current traffic conditions. Options (permitted): - A non-compliant traffic control device may be replaced in kind when engineering judgment indicates it is more appropriate because: (A) one compliant device in the midst of a series of adjacent non-compliant devices would be confusing to road users, and/or (B) the schedule for replacement of the whole series of non-compliant devices will result in achieving timely compliance with the MUTCD. Citations: - Section 1B.03 Standard §12 (p.6) - Section 1B.03 Option §13 (p.6) - Section 1B.03 Standard §5 (p.5) - Section 5A.04 Guidance §6 (p.762) - Section 6K.01 Standard §15 (p.815) - Section 1D.10 Guidance §1 (p.38)

**Gold answer:** The replacement must generally be a compliant device. A non-compliant device may be replaced in kind only when engineering judgment finds that a lone compliant device among adjacent non-compliant devices would confuse users and/or the planned replacement schedule for the whole series will achieve timely compliance.

Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: A non-compliant device or application may not be used on a covered public-travel facility without first receiving official experimentation approval from the FHWA Office of Transpo… Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. A traffic control device or application that does not comply with the MUTCD may not be used on any street, highway, bikeway, or site roadway open to public travel until official approval to experiment has first been received from FHWA's Office of Transportation Operations. This flows from the broader federal requirement that all devices on roads open to public travel be in substantial conformance with FHWA-issued or FHWA-endorsed standards. Standards (mandatory): - "A traffic control device or application that does not comply with the provisions of this Manual shall not be used on any street, highway, bikeway, or site roadway open to public travel (see definition in Section 1C.02) without first receiving official approval to experiment from the FHWA's Office of Transportation Operations." - "The U.S. Secretary of Transportation, under authority granted by the Highway Safety Act of 1966, decreed that traffic control devices on all streets and highways open to public travel in accordance with 23 U.S.C. 109(d) and 402(a) in each State shall be in substantial conformance with the Standards issued or endorsed by the FHWA." - "Before any highway, site roadway open to public travel (see definition in Section 1C.02), detour, or temporary route is opened to public travel, all traffic control devices necessary for safe operation shall be in place." Citations: - Section 1B.05 Standard §2 (p.6) - Section 1B.03 Standard §1 (p.5) - Section 1D.09 Standard §1 (p.38) Note (informational, non-normative context): The MUTCD explains that 23 CFR 655.603 likewise requires substantial conformance with FHWA-endorsed standards; that FHWA may, in certain circumstances, allow an effective experimental device to remain in use after an experiment ends (typically when interim approval is being considered); and that interim approval permits optional use under agreed conditions until rulemaking occurs — none of which authorizes use of a non-compliant device before approval to experiment is received (Section 1B.03 Support §2 (p.5); Section 1B.05 Support §15 (p.9); Section 1B.07 Support §3 (p.11)).

**Gold answer:** No. A non-compliant device or application may not be used on a covered public-travel facility without first receiving official experimentation approval from the FHWA Office of Transportation Operations.

Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1B.05, p.9, p.8. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Failure to submit two consecutive semi-annual progress reports terminates the experiment and rescinds FHWA approval, requiring restoration of the sites to MUTCD-compliant conditio… | The final report must be submitted within 3 months after the experiment concludes. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.500. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The experimenting agency must submit a final report within 3 months of the conclusion of an approved experiment; if no final report is received and no mitigating circumstances are reported within 6 months of the end of the experimentation period, the experiment is terminated and FHWA approval is rescinded. Similarly, failure to submit two consecutive semi-annual progress reports terminates the experiment and rescinds FHWA approval. In either case, the site(s) must be restored to a condition complying with the MUTCD within 3 months. Standards (mandatory): - "The experimenting agency shall submit a final report within 3 months of the conclusion of an approved experiment. If a final report is not received by the FHWA's Office of Transportation Operations, and the experimenting agency fails to notify the FHWA of any mitigating circumstances within 6 months of the end of the approved experimentation period, then the experiment shall be considered terminated and shall constitute rescission of the FHWA's approval to the experimenting agency, requiring restoration of the site(s) to a condition that complies with the provisions of this Manual within 3 months." - "The experimenting agency shall submit a semi-annual progress report for any approved experiment even if no work was performed during the previous reporting period. Failure to submit two consecutive progress reports shall result in termination of the experiment and shall constitute rescission of the FHWA's approval to the experimenting agency, requiring restoration of the site(s) to a condition that complies with the provisions of this Manual within 3 months." - "The required semi-annual progress reports shall be submitted throughout the course of an approved experiment in accordance with the following schedule: A. No later than August 1st for the preceding period of January through June; and B. No later than February 1st for the preceding period of July through December." Citations: - Section 1B.05 Standard §14 (p.9) - Section 1B.05 Standard §13 (p.8) - Section 1B.05 Standard §12 (p.8)

**Gold answer:** The final report must be submitted within 3 months after the experiment concludes. Failure to submit two consecutive semi-annual progress reports terminates the experiment and rescinds FHWA approval, requiring restoration of the sites to MUTCD-compliant conditions within 3 months.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A new traffic control device must be in the public domain to be eligible for experimentation or interim approval. Any proprietary protection—patents, trademarks, or copyrights—must be expressly abandoned, and the experimenting agency, manufacturer/developer, and supplier must all provide a legally-binding certification that the device is unprotected and freely usable, acknowledging that future proprietary protection would cancel the approval or experimentation authorization. Standards (mandatory): - "A traffic control device design or application shall not be eligible for official experimentation (see Section 1B.05) or interim approval (see Section 1B.07) unless it is in the public domain. Express abandonment of any and all forms of proprietary protection, such as patents, trademarks, or copyrights, related to the design and application of the traffic control device shall satisfy the requirement for the traffic control device to be in the public domain." - A request for experimentation involving a new device or new application "shall include from the agency conducting the experiment, the manufacturer and/or developer of the device, and the supplier of the device, a legally-binding statement certifying that the traffic control device is not protected by a patent, trademark, or copyright in accordance with Section 1D.06, and that the traffic control device is in the public domain and can be used freely in traffic control device design and application without infringement or claim of trade secret misappropriation." - The legally-binding statement "shall also state that the agency conducting the experiment, the manufacturer and/or developer of the device, and the supplier of the device are aware that if patent, trademark, or copyright protection is established in the future for the device or application, such action will result in its removal from the MUTCD, cancellation of its interim approval, or cancellation of the authorization for experimentation." - The request for permission to experiment shall contain, among other elements, "a legally-binding statement that the experimental device or application is in the public domain, in accordance with Paragraph 16 of this Section." Citations: - Section 1D.06 Standard §2 (p.34) - Section 1B.05 Standard §16 (p.9) - Section 1B.05 Standard §10 (p.8) - Section 1B.07 Support §1 (p.9) - Section 1B.05 Support §1 (p.6)

**Gold answer:** The traffic-control message, design, or application must be in the public domain and not protected by patent, trademark, or copyright. Legally binding statements are required from the relevant agency/developer/supplier for experimentation. Protection of internal components or manufacturing methods can still be allowed.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Under Section 1D.01, an effective traffic control device should have five characteristics: it should fulfill a need, command attention, convey a clear and simple meaning, command respect from road users, and give adequate time for proper response. These are Guidance (recommended) principles, not mandatory Standards, and other MUTCD sections (such as those on changeable message signs and device placement) point back to these principles. Guidance (recommended): - To be effective, a traffic control device should: A. Fulfill a need; B. Command attention; C. Convey a clear, simple meaning; D. Command respect from road users; and E. Give adequate time for proper response. - Design, placement, operation, maintenance, and uniformity should be carefully considered to maximize a device's ability to be consistent with the five principles; vehicle speed and road-user types should also be carefully considered as elements governing design, operation, placement, and location of devices. - The proper use of traffic control devices should provide the road user with the information necessary to safely, efficiently, and lawfully use the streets, highways, pedestrian facilities, and bikeways. - In developing messages for display on CMS, the provisions of Section 1D.01 should be consulted for the principles of an effective traffic control device. - Placement of a device should be within the road user's view for adequate visibility, appropriately positioned with respect to what it applies to, and located and legible so that a road user has adequate time to respond properly in both day and night conditions. - Devices should be placed and operated in a uniform and consistent manner as part of maintaining uniformity in traffic control. Citations: - Section 1D.01 Guidance §3 (p.31) - Section 1D.01 Guidance §4 (p.31) - Section 1D.01 Guidance §5 (p.31) - Section 2L.04 Guidance §3 (p.513) - Section 1D.09 Guidance §3 (p.38) - Section 1D.09 Guidance §4 (p.38)

**Gold answer:** It should fulfill a need, command attention, convey a clear and simple meaning, command respect from road users, and give adequate time for proper response.

**Gold answer:** Fluorescent pink is for incident management, orange is for temporary traffic control, and purple is restricted to vehicles with registered electronic toll collection accounts.

The same prohibition applies specifically to changeable message signs (CMS), their supports, and other equipment. Additionally, States are encouraged to restrict outdoor advertising from resembling traffic control devices. Standards (mandatory): - "Traffic control devices or their supports shall not bear any advertising message or any other message that is not related to traffic control." - "CMS shall display only traffic operational, regulatory, warning, and guidance information except as otherwise provided in this Chapter. Advertising or other messages not related to traffic control shall not be displayed on a CMS or on its supports or other equipment." - "Traffic control devices, public announcements or notices, and other signs or messages within the highway right-of-way shall be placed only as authorized by a public authority or the official having jurisdiction, or, in the case of site roadways or private toll roads open to public travel, by the private owner or private official having jurisdiction, for the purpose of regulating, warning, or guiding traffic." Citations: - Section 1D.07 Standard §1 (p.34) - Section 2L.02 Standard §1 (p.511) - Section 1D.02 Standard §3 (p.31) - Section 1D.02 Support §6 (p.32)

**Gold answer:** No. They may not bear advertising or any message unrelated to traffic control. Acknowledgment signs, Specific Service signs, and Tourist-Oriented Directional signs are not treated as advertising under this provision.

## T016

**Question:** How does the MUTCD classify regulatory, warning, and guide signs by function?

The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2D.39, Section 2A.03, Section 2A.13, Section 2F.12, Section 2G.10, Section 2L.01. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Regulatory signs give notice of traffic laws or regulations. | Warning signs give notice of a situation that might not be readily apparent. | Guide signs show route designations, destinations, directions, distances, services, points of interest, or other geographical, recreational, or cultural information. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The MUTCD classifies signs by function into three principal categories: regulatory signs, which give notice of traffic laws or regulations; warning signs, which give notice of situations that might not be readily apparent; and guide signs, which convey route, destination, direction, distance, service, and similar geographical, recreational, or cultural information. This functional classification is a mandatory (Standard) definition, and it carries through the Manual—for example, when regulatory or warning messages are incorporated within guide signs, they must retain their functional color coding. Standards (mandatory): - "Signs shall be defined by their function as follows: A. Regulatory signs give notice of traffic laws or regulations. B. Warning signs give notice of a situation that might not be readily apparent. C. Guide signs show route designations, destinations, directions, distances, services, points of interest, and other geographical, recreational, or cultural information." - "When regulatory messages are incorporated within a guide sign, they shall be on a rectangular panel with a black legend on a white background. When warning messages are incorporated within a guide sign, they shall be on a rectangular panel with a black legend on a yellow background." - "A combination of guide and regulatory signs shall be used in advance of and at the initial entry point and all intermediate entry points from general-purpose lanes or facilities to contiguous, barrier-separated, and buffer-separated preferential lanes where access between the preferential and general-purpose lanes is restricted to designated locations." Guidance (recommended): - "Signs should be individually installed on separate posts or mountings except where: A. One sign supplements another..." — i.e., signs of different functions are generally installed separately, with limited exceptions such as supplemental plaques. Support (informational): - Changeable message signs remain traffic control devices at all times, and their regulatory, warning, and guidance messages fall under the same MUTCD provisions as the corresponding conventional sign categories. - Examples of regulatory and warning signs applied together (with cross-references to guide signs in Section 2D.39) are shown for roundabouts and neighborhood traffic circles in Figures 2B-21 through 2B-24. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 2A-2 illustrates mounting heights and lateral offsets for the functional sign classes, including a regulatory STOP sign (A), warning signs with advisory speed plaques (B, D), a route assembly (E), and guide signs on freeways and overhead structures (G, H). [Image 2] Figure 2A-3 shows typical placements of regulatory STOP and YIELD signs at various intersection configurations. [Image 3] Figure 2A-4 relates the chapters (2B, 2C, and following) that cover regulatory, warning, and guide signs. [Image 4] Table 2C-3 provides advance placement guidance specific to the warning sign category. [Image 5] shows the table of contents for Chapter 6 covering TTC zone regulatory (6G) and warning (6H) signs, reflecting the same functional classification in temporary traffic control. [Image 6] shows the table of contents listing Chapter 2B regulatory sign sections and Chapter 2C "Warning Signs and Object Markers," demonstrating the Manual's organization by sign function. [Image 7] shows Table 2A-2, which assigns distinct legend/background color combinations to regulatory, warning, and guide sign types. [Image 8] shows Figure 2B-23, an example of regulatory and warning signs at a two-lane roundabout with cross-reference to guide signs in Section 2D.39. Citations: - Section 2A.03 Standard §1 (p.40) - Section 2A.13 Guidance §6 (p.57) - Section 2F.12 Standard §11 (p.392) - Section 2G.10 Standard §12 (p.422) - Section 2L.01 Support §3 (p.510) - Section 2B.51 Support §6 (p.123) - Figure 2A-2 (p.51) - Figure 2A-3 (p.52) - Figure 2A-4 (p.50) - Table 2C-3 (p.152) - Page 23 - Page 9 - Page 85 - Page 162

**Gold answer:** Regulatory signs give notice of traffic laws or regulations. Warning signs give notice of a situation that might not be readily apparent. Guide signs show route designations, destinations, directions, distances, services, points of interest, or other geographical, recreational, or cultural information.

**Question:** What casing and orientation rules generally apply to word messages on signs?

**Grade:** C=0.60, Comp=0.50, F=0.70, Cit=0.50, Strict full credit=No

**Descriptive mean:** 57.5% (Limited credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 90%

The official rubric did not award strict full credit because at least one dimension scored below 1.00. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response contains part of the correct conclusion, but it also omits or misstates a material requirement. Completeness rationale: The audit found incomplete coverage even though the automated clause comparison did not isolate a single missing sentence. Faithfulness rationale: The faithfulness deduction follows the adjudicated issue described below. Citation rationale: The response provides some evidence localization, but the audit judged it incomplete, imprecise, or partly incorrect. The response cites: Section 2A.04, Section 1A.05, Section 2D.04, Section 2A.09, Section 2A.08, Section 2D.55. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Word messages generally read horizontally from left to right. | Place, street, and highway names use initial upper-case letters with lower-case letters Adjudication note: Correct left-to-right orientation but substitutes a community-wayfinding casing rule for the general sign-message casing rule. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.832 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: In general, word messages on signs must be aligned horizontally and read left to right, and must use the standard wording shown in the MUTCD and the "Standard Highway Signs" publication. Casing rules vary by sign type: on community wayfinding guide signs (as on guide signs generally), destination names must use lower-case letters with initial upper-case letters, while all other word messages must be in all upper-case letters. Minor modifications to word message layout are allowed, but shapes and colors must remain as standardized. Standards (mandatory): - "Except as otherwise provided in this Manual, all word messages shall be aligned horizontally across a sign, reading left to right." - "Except as provided in Section 2A.04, all word messages shall use standard wording as shown in this Manual and in the 'Standard Highway Signs' publication (see Section 1A.05)." - "The lettering for destinations on community wayfinding guide signs shall be a combination of lower-case letters with initial upper-case letters (see Section 2D.04). All other word messages on community wayfinding guide signs shall be in all upper-case letters." - "Standardization of these designs does not preclude further improvement by minor modifications to the orientation of symbols (see Section 2A.09), width of borders, or layout of word messages, but all shapes and colors shall be as indicated." Guidance (recommended): - "Word messages should be as brief as practical and the lettering should be large enough to provide the necessary legibility distance." - For pavement marking word messages (a related orientation rule): "If a pavement marking word message consists of more than one line of information, it should read in the direction of travel. The first word of the message should be nearest to the road user." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Table 2L-3 shows CMS message examples displayed in all upper-case lettering, with a note that single-color CMS with pixel spacing greater than 20 mm use all upper-case lettering, while multi-color full-matrix CMS with pixel spacing of 20 mm or less should use upper- and lower-case lettering where appropriate. [Image 2] Figure 9D-5 accompanies shared-use path provisions requiring destination lettering to combine lower-case letters with initial upper-case letters, with all other word messages in all upper-case letters — paralleling the community wayfinding casing rule. Citations: - Section 2A.08 Standard §1 (p.45) - Section 2A.08 Standard §2 (p.45) - Section 2D.55 Standard §32 (p.271) - Section 2A.04 Standard §8 (p.40) - Section 2D.55 Guidance §28 (p.271) - Section 3B.21 Guidance §2 (p.575) - Table 2L-3 (p.517) - Figure 9D-5 (p.1082)

**Gold answer:** Word messages generally read horizontally from left to right. Place, street, and highway names use initial upper-case letters with lower-case letters; other legends use upper-case letters unless another MUTCD provision specifies otherwise.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A NEW plaque used to call attention to a new sign should remain in place for a period determined by engineering judgment, but not more than 12 months, and should be removed no later than 12 months after installation. Strobe lights are prohibited as a means of enhancing sign conspicuity. Standards (mandatory): - "Strobe lights shall not be used to enhance the conspicuity of highway signs." Guidance (recommended): - If a NEW plaque is used, it should remain in place for a period of time determined by engineering judgment, but not more than 12 months. - The NEW plaque should be removed no later than 12 months after it was installed. Options (permitted): - A NEW plaque may be added above a new standard regulatory or warning sign, for a period of time in accordance with the guidance above, to call attention to the new sign, based on engineering judgment as one of several permitted methods of enhancing sign conspicuity. - A NEW (W16-15P) plaque may be mounted above a regulatory sign when a new regulation takes effect to alert road users to the new traffic regulation, and may also be mounted above an advance warning sign (such as a Signal Ahead sign for a newly-installed traffic control signal) to warn of a new traffic condition. Citations: - Section 2A.11 Guidance §3 (p.49) - Section 2C.60 Guidance §2 (p.197) - Section 2A.11 Standard §4 (p.49) - Section 2A.11 Option §1 (p.49) - Section 2C.60 Option §1 (p.197)

**Gold answer:** The NEW plaque should remain only for a period determined by engineering judgment and no more than 12 months. Strobe lights may not be used to enhance highway-sign conspicuity.

The response identifies: Section 6F.02, Section 2A.15, Section 8B.04, Figure 2A-2, Figure 6F-1, Figure 2M-3. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The minimum is 5 feet in rural areas and 7 feet in business, commercial, or residential areas where such activity or obstruction is likely, measured as specified from the near pav… Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: In rural areas, roadside signs must be mounted at least 5 feet, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement. In business, commercial, or residential areas where parking, bicyclist, or pedestrian movements are likely to occur (or where the view of the sign might be obstructed), the minimum height is 7 feet, measured from the bottom of the sign to the top of the curb, or in the absence of curb, to the elevation of the near edge of the traveled way. The same 5-foot/7-foot distinction applies in temporary traffic control zones and at passive grade crossings. Standards (mandatory): - "In rural areas, the minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement, of signs installed at the side of the road shall be 5 feet (see Figure 2A-2)." - "In business, commercial, or residential areas where parking, bicyclist, or pedestrian movements are likely to occur, or where the view of the sign might be obstructed, the minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the top of the curb, or in the absence of curb, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the traveled way, of signs installed at the side of the road shall be 7 feet (see Figure 2A-2)." - For temporary traffic control zones: "The minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement, of signs installed at the side of the road in rural areas shall be 5 feet (see Figure 6F-1)," and in business, commercial, or residential areas where parking or pedestrian movements are likely to occur or where the view of the sign might be obstructed, the minimum height "shall be 7 feet (see Figure 6F-1)." - At passive grade crossings, when a Crossbuck Assembly is installed on a new support or a YIELD/STOP sign is on a separate support, the mounting height "shall be at least 5 feet in rural areas and shall be at least 7 feet in areas where parking or pedestrian movements are likely to occur." Guidance (recommended): - Except as provided in Paragraph 12 of Section 6F.02, signs mounted on portable sign supports that do not meet the minimum mounting heights provided in Part 2 should not be used for a duration of more than 3 days. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2A-2 illustrates typical installations, showing "5 ft MIN." for roadside signs in rural areas (diagrams A, B, E) and "7 ft MIN." with a 2 ft lateral offset for a roadside sign in a business, commercial, or residential area (diagram C). - [Image 2] Page 831 (Figure 2A-2 as reproduced) shows the same rural 5-foot minimum and 7-foot minimum in business, commercial, or residential areas, with the 7 ft asterisked note "Where parking or pedestrian movements are likely to occur." - [Image 3] Page 92 shows Section 2A.15 Mounting Height text with the Standard provisions establishing the 5-foot rural minimum (§4) and the 7-foot minimum in business, commercial, or residential areas (§5). - [Image 4] Page 99 shows Section 6F.02 text and Figure 6F-1, depicting temporary traffic control signs with "5 ft MIN." in rural areas (diagrams A and B) and "7 ft MIN." in business, commercial, or residential areas with and without curb (diagrams C and D). - [Image 5] Page 566 (Figure 2M-3) shows recreational/cultural interest sign assemblies mounted at "7 ft MIN." in business, commercial, or residential areas and "5 ft MIN." in rural areas, consistent with the general mounting height standards. Citations: - Section 2A.15 Standard §4 (p.58) - Section 2A.15 Standard §5 (p.58) - Section 6F.02 Standard §4 (p.790) - Section 6F.02 Standard §5 (p.790) - Section 8B.04 Standard §12 (p.1000) - Section 6F.02 Guidance §11 (p.791) - Figure 2A-2 (p.51) - Page 831 - Page 92 - Page 99 - Page 566

**Gold answer:** The minimum is 5 feet in rural areas and 7 feet in business, commercial, or residential areas where such activity or obstruction is likely, measured as specified from the near pavement edge or curb.

The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Overhead signs must provide a vertical clearance of not less than 17 feet, measured to the sign, light fixture, or sign bridge, over the entire width of the pavement and shoulders. Reduced clearances are permitted only where nearby structures have lesser vertical clearance (or in special cases such as tunnels), and certain preferential-lane signs on median barriers may be mounted higher so that at least 17 feet of clearance is maintained. Standards (mandatory): - "Overhead signs shall provide a vertical clearance of not less than 17 feet to the sign, light fixture, or sign bridge over the entire width of the pavement and shoulders, except where the structure on which the overhead signs are to be mounted or other structures along the roadway near the sign structure have a lesser vertical clearance." (shown in [Image 2]) - "Signs that are post-mounted on a median barrier that overhang any portion of the traveled way shall be mounted with a vertical clearance that complies with that of overhead signs." (shown in [Image 2]) Options (permitted): - If the vertical clearance of other structures along the roadway near the sign structure is less than 16 feet, the vertical clearance to an overhead sign structure or support may be as low as 1 foot higher than the vertical clearance of the other structures in order to improve the visibility of the overhead signs (shown in [Image 2]). - Where lateral clearance is limited, Preferential Lane regulatory signs post-mounted on a median barrier that are 72 inches or less in width may be mounted higher, "such that the vertical clearance to the bottom of the sign, light fixture, or structural support, whichever is lowest, is not less than 17 feet above any portion of the pavement and shoulders." - Where lateral clearance is limited, warning signs applicable only to a preferential lane on a median barrier may likewise be mounted higher so that the vertical clearance "is not less than 17 feet above any portion of the pavement and shoulders." - The same 17-foot minimum applies where Preferential Lane guide signs 72 inches or less in width are mounted higher due to limited lateral clearance. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2A-2, panel H ("Overhead sign"), illustrates an overhead-mounted street name sign with a "17 ft MIN." vertical clearance dimension above the roadway and a 6-foot lateral offset for the support. - [Image 2] Contains the governing Standard text requiring 17 feet of vertical clearance for overhead signs over the entire width of the pavement and shoulders, along with the Options for reduced clearance near lower structures. - [Image 3] Shows examples of overhead guide signs at an intersection (Figure 2D-10, Sheet 2), illustrating the type of overhead installations to which the clearance requirement applies. - [Image 4] Shows overhead Arrow-per-Lane and conventional guide signs at an intersection (Figure 2D-10, Sheet 1), further illustrating overhead sign installations. - [Image 5] Section 2E.19 (Overhead Sign Installations) notes that "Vertical clearance of overhead signs is discussed in Section 2A.15." Citations: - Figure 2A-2 (p.51) - Page 100 - Page 344 - Section 2G.03 Option §14 (p.411) - Section 2G.08 Option §4 (p.419) - Section 2G.10 Option §18 (p.422)

**Gold answer:** At least 17 feet, except where nearby structures or special constrained structures qualify for the specific exceptions in Section 2A.15.

Plaques such as 2-WAY, 3-WAY, or 4-WAY are expressly prohibited with STOP signs. Standards (mandatory): - "At intersections where all approaches are controlled by STOP signs (see Section 2B.12), an ALL-WAY (R1-3P) supplemental plaque (see Figure 2B-1) shall be mounted below each STOP sign. The ALL-WAY plaque shall have a white legend and border on a red background." - "Supplemental plaques with legends such as 2-WAY, 3-WAY, 4-WAY, or other numbers of ways shall not be used with STOP signs." Guidance (recommended): - Where STOP signs control all but one approach (i.e., not an all-way stop), "The TRAFFIC FROM LEFT (RIGHT) DOES NOT STOP (W4-4aP) plaque or ONCOMING TRAFFIC DOES NOT STOP (W4-4bP) plaque should be used... unless the only non-stopped approach is from a one-way street." Options (permitted): - The CROSS TRAFFIC DOES NOT STOP (W4-4P) plaque may be used with a STOP sign when engineering judgment indicates road users could misinterpret the intersection as an all-way stop. - The W4-4aP or W4-4bP plaque may be used when such messages more accurately describe the traffic controls established at the intersection. Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Figure 2B-1, which depicts the ALL WAY (R1-3P) plaque — a red plaque with white legend and border — alongside the STOP sign (R1-1). [Image 3] shows Section 2B.04 in full, including the Standard requiring the ALL-WAY plaque at all-way stop intersections and the prohibition of numbered-way plaques, together with Figure 2B-1. Citations: - Section 2B.04 Standard §4 (p.74) - Section 2B.04 Standard §5 (p.74) - Section 2B.04 Guidance §7 (p.74) - Section 2C.66 Guidance §3 (p.198) - Section 2C.66 Option §1 (p.198) - Section 2C.66 Option §2 (p.198) - Figure 2B-1 (p.74)

**Gold answer:** An ALL-WAY (R1-3P) plaque must be mounted below every STOP sign. Numeric-way plaques such as 2-WAY, 3-WAY, or 4-WAY are not permitted with STOP signs.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: YIELD signs are mandatory at roundabout entrances to require entering road users to yield the right-of-way to traffic in the roundabout. They must control the approach roadways only — the MUTCD expressly prohibits using YIELD signs to control the circulatory roadway itself. Standards (mandatory): - "A YIELD sign shall be used to require road users to yield the right-of-way to other traffic at the entrance to a roundabout. YIELD signs at roundabouts shall be used to control the approach roadways and shall not be used to control the circulatory roadway." Guidance (recommended): - On an approach to a circular intersection controlled by a YIELD sign and at uncontrolled exits, crosswalks should be a minimum of 20 feet from the edge of the circulatory roadway. Options (permitted): - YIELD (word) and YIELD AHEAD (symbol or word) pavement markings may be used on approaches to roundabouts. - At roundabouts where ONE WAY signs have been installed in the central island, Roundabout Circulation (R6-5P) plaques may be placed below the YIELD signs on approaches to supplement the central island signs. - The Vehicular Traffic Warning sign assembly may be installed on an approach to a circular intersection controlled by a YIELD sign where the crosswalk is at least 20 feet in advance of the yield point at the entrance to the circulatory roadway. - The Non-Vehicular Warning sign assembly may likewise be installed on such a YIELD-controlled approach where the crosswalk is at least 20 feet in advance of the yield point. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 3D-1 shows YIELD word markings and yield lines placed on each approach roadway (not within the circulatory roadway), with crosswalks set back a minimum of 20 feet from the circulatory roadway edge. [Image 2] The page containing Sections 3D.04–3D.07 documents that a yield line may indicate the point behind which vehicles are required to yield at the entrance to a roundabout, and that YIELD word markings may be used on approaches. [Image 3] The page containing Section 2B.51 shows that Roundabout Circulation plaques may be placed below YIELD signs on roundabout approaches to supplement central island signs. [Image 4] The page containing Section 2B.10 shows the Standard text mandating YIELD signs at roundabout entrances to control approach roadways and prohibiting their use on the circulatory roadway. [Image 5] The page containing Section 3C.09 shows the guidance that crosswalks on YIELD-controlled approaches to circular intersections should be a minimum of 20 feet from the edge of the circulatory roadway. Citations: - Section 2B.10 Standard §5 (p.78) - Section 3C.09 Guidance §3 (p.593) - Section 3D.05 Option §1 (p.605) - Section 2B.51 Option §4 (p.118) - Section 2C.54 Option §11 (p.192) - Section 2C.55 Option §6 (p.193) - Figure 3D-1 (p.596) - Page 646 - Page 159 - Page 119 - Page 634

**Gold answer:** A YIELD sign must control each entering approach roadway. It must not be used to control the circulatory roadway.

What the evidence does establish is the speed-based adjustment: when the major-street 85th-percentile approach speed exceeds 40 mph, those minimum vehicular volume warrants may be reduced to 70 percent of their stated values. Options (permitted): - "If the 85th-percentile approach speed of the major-street traffic exceeds 40 mph, the minimum vehicular volume warrants may be reduced to 70 percent of the values given in Items A and B in Paragraph 1 of this Section." Citations: - Section 2B.16 Option §2 (p.79) Note: The remaining evidence (Sections 4C.02, 4C.03, 4C.05, and 4C.08) concerns traffic signal warrants, not All-Way Stop Control Warrant D, and does not supply the missing threshold values.

**Gold answer:** For the same 8 hours of a typical day, the combined entering volume must be at least 300 units per hour on the major-street approaches and at least 200 units per hour on the minor-street approaches. If the major-street 85th-percentile speed exceeds 40 mph, the minimums may be reduced to 70 percent of those values.

Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: It must display the speed limit established by law, ordinance, regulation, or an authorized agency based on an engineering study, in multiples of 5 mph. | It should not be used for an advisory speed | an advisory speed plaque below an appropriate warning sign should be used instead. **Model answer:** Direct Answer: A Speed Limit (R2-1) sign must display the limit established by law, ordinance, regulation, or as adopted by the authorized agency based on an engineering study, and the displayed limits must be in multiples of 5 mph. A Speed Limit sign should not be used to show an advisory speed for a roadway condition; instead, an Advisory Speed plaque mounted below a warning sign is the recommended device for that purpose. Standards (mandatory): - "The Speed Limit (R2-1) sign (see Figure 2B-3) shall display the limit established by law, ordinance, regulation, or as adopted by the authorized agency based on an engineering study. The speed limits displayed shall be in multiples of 5 mph." - For variable speed limit applications, "The regulatory speed displayed on a variable Speed Limit sign shall comply with Paragraph 13 of" Section 2B.21 (i.e., the same display and 5-mph-multiple requirements apply). Guidance (recommended): - As shown in the page text of [Image 3]: "An advisory speed plaque (see Section 2C.59) mounted below a warning sign should be used to warn road users of an advisory speed for a roadway condition. A Speed Limit sign should not be used for this purpose." Options (permitted): - An Advisory Speed plaque may be used (with the PAVEMENT ENDS warning sign) "when the change in roadway condition requires a reduced speed." - An Advisory Exit Speed or Advisory Ramp Speed sign may be used based on engineering judgment when the difference between the mainline speed limit and the exit or ramp advisory speed in the vicinity of the departure is 10 mph or less. - A Speed Limit Sign Beacon may be used with a fixed or variable Speed Limit sign, and a flashing beacon (with an appropriate accompanying sign) may be used to indicate that the displayed speed limit is in effect. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] shows Figure 2B-3, including the R2-1 SPEED LIMIT 50 sign and related speed limit signs/plaques, all displaying speeds in multiples of 5 mph. - [Image 2] shows warning signs for a horizontal curve where the advisory speed (35 MPH) is conveyed by W13-1P advisory speed plaques mounted below warning signs — not by a Speed Limit sign. - [Image 3] shows the Section 2B.21 text, including the Standard that the R2-1 sign "shall display the limit established by law..." in "multiples of 5 mph" and the Guidance that "A Speed Limit sign should not be used" to convey an advisory speed. - [Image 4] shows the full Figure 2B-3 page of Speed Limit Signs and Plaques with all displayed limits in multiples of 5 mph. - [Image 5] shows enhanced conspicuity treatments applied to a SPEED LIMIT 35 sign (flags) as regulatory applications, distinct from yellow advisory speed displays (e.g., the EXIT 25 MPH warning panel). Citations: - Section 2B.21 Standard §13 (p.85) - Section 2G.26 Standard §3 (p.457) - Section 2C.28 Option §2 (p.173) - Section 2C.12 Option §5 (p.160) - Section 4S.04 Option §3 (p.753) - Figure 2B-3 (p.86) - Page 126

**Gold answer:** It must display the speed limit established by law, ordinance, regulation, or an authorized agency based on an engineering study, in multiples of 5 mph. It should not be used for an advisory speed; an advisory speed plaque below an appropriate warning sign should be used instead.

The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2C.04, Section 6H.02, Section 6B.04, Table 2C-3, Table 6B-1, Figure 2C-2. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Warning signs should be placed to provide adequate perception-response time. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 4, which produced MRR=0.250. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.501 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Advance placement of warning signs is determined by providing drivers adequate Perception-Response Time, using the distances in Table 2C-3 (based on posted or 85th-percentile speed and whether the condition requires stopping, deceleration, or lane changing) applied with engineering judgment. For temporary traffic control zones, warning signs should be placed in advance of the transition and activity areas at distances that vary with roadway type, condition, and posted speed, per Table 6B-1 — from roughly 100 feet on low-speed urban streets to ½ mile or more on freeways and expressways. All of these provisions are Guidance (recommended), not mandatory Standards. Guidance (recommended): - Warning signs should be placed so that they provide an adequate PRT; the distances contained in Table 2C-3 should be applied with engineering judgment, and the effectiveness of the placement of warning signs should be periodically evaluated under both day and night conditions. - Per Table 2C-3, advance placement distances range from 225 ft (Condition A) and 115 ft (Condition B, stop condition) at 20 mph up to 1,600 ft and 1,010 ft at 85 mph; for warning signs with less than 6-inch legend or more than four words, a minimum of 100 feet should be added to the advance placement distance to provide adequate legibility. - In TTC zones, where highway conditions permit, warning signs should be placed in advance of the transition and activity areas at varying distances depending on roadway type, condition, and posted speed; where a series of two or more advance warning signs is used, the closest sign should be placed approximately 100 feet for low-speed urban streets to 1,000 feet or more for freeways and expressways (see Table 6B-1). - On freeways and expressways, advance warning sign placement should be longer because drivers are conditioned to uninterrupted flow, extending as far as ½ mile or more. - On urban streets, the effective placement of the nearest warning sign to the TTC zone should range from 4 to 8 times the speed limit in mph, with the high end used when speeds are relatively high; with two or more signs on higher-speed streets, the advance warning area should extend a greater distance. - On rural highways, the effective placement of the first warning sign should be substantially longer—from 8 to 12 times the speed limit in mph—and the advance warning area should extend 1,500 feet or more for open highway conditions. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Table 2C-3 shows advance placement distances by posted or 85th-percentile speed for Condition A (speed reduction/lane changing in heavy traffic) and Condition B (deceleration to listed advisory speeds), with footnotes on legibility adjustments and minimum 100-ft spacing. - [Image 2] Table 6B-1 (Warning Sign Minimum Spacing) gives A, B, and C sign-spacing dimensions by road type: 100 feet for low-speed urban, 350 feet for high-speed urban, 500 feet for rural, and 1,000/1,500/2,640 feet for expressways/freeways. - [Image 3] Page 193 shows Section 2C.04 (Placement of Warning Signs) with its Guidance on providing adequate PRT and applying Table 2C-3 with engineering judgment, together with the full Table 2C-3. - [Image 4] Page 839 shows Section 6H.02 (Position of Advance Warning Signs) Guidance that signs be placed at varying distances by roadway type, condition, and posted speed, per Table 6B-1. - [Image 5] Page 197 shows Figure 2C-2 (curve warning sign examples), whose notes reference Table 2C-3 for advance placement distance guidelines. - [Image 6] Page 812 shows Section 6B.04 (Advance Warning Area) with the Guidance on freeway/expressway, urban street (4–8 times speed limit), and rural highway (8–12 times speed limit) placement. Citations: - Section 2C.04 Guidance §5 (p.152) - Section 2C.04 Guidance §804 (p.153) - Section 6H.02 Guidance §1 (p.798) - Section 6B.04 Guidance §3 (p.771) - Section 6B.04 Guidance §4 (p.771) - Section 6B.04 Guidance §6 (p.771) - Table 2C-3 (p.152) - Table 6B-1 (p.773) - Page 193 - Page 839 - Page 197 - Page 812

**Gold answer:** Warning signs should be placed to provide adequate perception-response time. Table 2C-3 is an aid, but its distances should be applied with engineering judgment and can be adjusted for roadway features, other signing, and visibility.

Guidance (recommended): - The Chevron Alignment signs should be visible for a sufficient distance to provide the road user with adequate time to react to the change in alignment. - When the Hairpin Curve sign or the 270-degree Loop sign is installed, either a One-Direction Large Arrow (W1-6) sign or Chevron Alignment (W1-8) signs should be installed on the outside of the turn or curve. Options (permitted): - Chevron Alignment signs may be used instead of or in addition to standard delineators. **Model answer:** Direct Answer: In the MUTCD, a unit of information on a changeable message sign is a single answer to a single question that a driver can use to make a decision (for example, "What happened?" → MAJOR CRASH). As a matter of guidance, a unit of information should not contain more than four words. Note that this definition and word limit are stated as Guidance ("should"), not as a mandatory Standard. Guidance (recommended): - "A unit of information, which is a single answer to a single question that a driver can use to make a decision, should not be more than four words." - Related principles: no more than three units of information should be displayed in a message phase; no more than four units of information should be in a message when traffic operating speeds are 35 mph or more; no more than five units when speeds are less than 35 mph; and only one unit of information should appear on each line of the CMS. Options (permitted): - A unit of information consisting of more than one word may be displayed on more than one line, and an additional downstream CMS may be used so the entire message can be read twice. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Table 2L-2 illustrates the concept by pairing four driver questions ("What happened?", "Where?", "Who is the advisory for?", "What is advised?") with four answers (MAJOR CRASH, AT EXIT 12, TO NEW YORK drivers, USE ROUTE 46), each counted as one unit of information, and shows how they form a two-phase message. Citations: - Section 2L.05 Guidance §1 (p.514) - Section 2L.05 Guidance §7 (p.516) - Section 2L.05 Option §9 (p.516) - Section 2L.05 Support §2 (p.515) - Table 2L-2 (p.515)

**Gold answer:** A unit of information is one answer to one question that a driver can use to make a decision. It should contain no more than four words; total message length should be governed by the number of information units and the size of the CMS.

The response identifies: Section 3B.04, Section 3B.05, Section 3B.01, Section 3B.06, Section 9E.13, Figure 3B-1. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Yellow center lines separate lanes traveling in opposite directions. | White lane lines separate lanes traveling in the same direction. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000.
