The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Yellow center line markings communicate that traffic on either side of the line is traveling in opposite directions, while white lane line markings communicate that traffic on either side of the line is traveling in the same direction. In short, yellow separates opposing traffic flows and white separates same-direction traffic flows, with the pattern (broken vs. solid) further indicating whether crossing the line is permitted. Standards (mandatory): - "Center line pavement markings, when used, shall be the pavement markings used to delineate the separation of traffic lanes that have opposite directions of travel on a roadway and shall be yellow." - "When used, lane line pavement markings delineating the separation of traffic lanes that have the same direction of travel shall be white." - "The center line markings on two-lane, two-way roadways shall be one of the following as shown in Figure 3B-1: A. Two-direction passing zone markings consisting of a normal width broken yellow line where crossing the center line markings for passing with care is permitted for traffic traveling in either direction; B. One-direction no-passing zone markings consisting of a double yellow line, one of which is a normal width broken yellow line and the other is a normal width solid yellow line, where crossing the center line markings for passing with care is permitted for the traffic traveling adjacent to the broken line, but is prohibited for traffic traveling adjacent to the solid line; or C. Two-direction no-passing zone markings consisting of two normal width solid yellow lines where crossing the center line markings for passing is prohibited for traffic traveling in either direction." - "Except where a reversible lane (see Section 3B.04) or a two-way left-turn lane (see Section 3B.05) is present, the center line markings on undivided two-way roadways with four or more lanes for moving motor vehicle traffic always available shall be the two-direction no-passing zone markings consisting of normal width double solid yellow lines as shown in Figure 3B-2." Options (permitted): - Solid white lane line markings may be used: on approaches to intersections to separate contiguous through lanes; to separate through traffic lanes from auxiliary lanes, such as an added uphill truck lane; and on approaches to crosswalks across multi-lane roadways. - On shared-use paths wide enough for two lanes, a solid yellow center line may be used to separate the two directions of travel where passing or traveling to the left of the line is not permitted, and a broken yellow center line may be used where passing is permitted. Visual evidence: [Image 1] (Figure 3B-2) shows multi-lane, two-way roadways where double solid yellow lines separate the opposing directions (indicated by opposing black arrows) and white lane lines separate same-direction lanes. [Image 2] (Figure 3B-3) shows three-lane, two-way marking where yellow center lines (broken/solid combinations) divide opposing flows and a white broken lane line separates the two same-direction lanes. [Image 3] (Figure 3B-1) shows two-lane, two-way applications with broken and solid yellow center lines dividing opposing traffic, illustrating passing and no-passing zones. [Image 4] and [Image 5] repeat Figures 3B-2 and 3B-3 in the page context, reinforcing the yellow-opposing/white-same-direction distinction. [Image 6] (freeway lane-drop example) shows white lane lines used exclusively between same-direction lanes, with yellow only at the left edge of the directional roadway. [Image 7] shows tubular markers supplementing yellow center line markings between opposing directions and white lane line markings between same-direction lanes at a crosswalk. Citations: - Section 3B.01 Standard §1 (p.540) - Section 3B.06 Standard §1 (p.546) - Section 3B.01 Standard §4 (p.540) - Section 3B.01 Standard §6 (p.540) - Section 3B.06 Option §8 (p.548) - Section 9E.13 Option §1 (p.1106) - Figure 3B-1 (p.540) - Figure 3B-2 (p.541) - Figure 3B-3 (p.542) - Page 582 - Page 583 - Page 595 - Page 674

**Gold answer:** Yellow center lines separate lanes traveling in opposite directions. White lane lines separate lanes traveling in the same direction.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: On roadways with center line markings, no-passing-zone markings are mandatory at horizontal or vertical curves where passing sight distance falls below the minimum in Table 3B-1 for the 85th-percentile speed or the speed limit. On two-way, two-lane or three-lane roadways, no-passing zones must also be established where an engineering study shows passing must be prohibited. When successive no-passing zones are separated by less than 400 feet, it is recommended that the markings connect the zones. Standards (mandatory): - "On roadways with center line markings, no-passing zone markings shall be used at horizontal or vertical curves where the passing sight distance is less than the minimum shown in Table 3B-1 for the 85th-percentile speed or the speed limit." - "On two-way, two-lane or three-lane roadways where center line markings are installed, no-passing zones shall be established at vertical and horizontal curves and other locations where an engineering study indicates that passing must be prohibited because of inadequate sight distances or other special conditions." - "No-passing zone markings shall be used on: A. Two-way roadways at lane-reduction transitions (see Section 3B.12), B. Approaches to obstructions that must be passed on the right (see Section 3B.13), C. Approaches to grade crossings (see Section 8C.02), and D. Approaches to crosswalks." - "No-passing zones shall be marked by either the one-direction no-passing zone pavement markings or the two-direction no-passing zone pavement markings described in Section 3B.01 and shown in Figures 3B-1 and 3B-3." Guidance (recommended): - Where the distance between successive no-passing zones is less than 400 feet, no-passing zone markings should connect the zones. Citations: - Section 3B.03 Standard §4 (p.543) - Section 3B.03 Standard §3 (p.543) - Section 3B.03 Standard §2 (p.542) - Section 3B.03 Standard §1 (p.542) - Section 3B.03 Guidance §7 (p.543) - Section 2B.36 Support §3 (p.102)

**Gold answer:** They are required on roadways with center line markings where passing sight distance is below the minimum in Table 3B-1 for the relevant speed. If successive no-passing zones are less than 400 feet apart, the markings should connect the zones.

Guidance (recommended): - Transverse line crosswalk markings should be limited to locations controlled by traffic control signals or on approaches controlled by STOP or YIELD signs. - To provide contrast, a gap of at least one-half the width of the white transverse line used to establish the crosswalk, but not less than 6 inches, should be used between the white crosswalk lines and an aesthetic surface treatment, such as unmarked pavement or a black contrast line. Options (permitted): - High-visibility crosswalk markings may be used where additional conspicuity is desired for a crosswalk over transverse line crosswalk markings. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 3C-1 shows the dimensional limits: transverse lines 6–24 inches wide with a 6 ft minimum crosswalk width; longitudinal bars 12–60 inches long (not to exceed 2.5 times the bar width) and 12–24 inches wide; ladder designs combining 6–24 inch transverse lines with 12–24 inch bars; and bar pairs of 8–12 inch bars, with a footnote that the minimum crosswalk width shall be 8 feet where the posted speed limit is 40 mph or greater at a non-intersection crosswalk. - [Image 2] shows Section 3C.03 (Design of Crosswalk Markings) with the Standard provisions that crosswalk markings shall be white, transverse lines shall be 6–24 inches wide, and minimum crosswalk widths of 6 feet (8 feet at high-speed non-intersection crosswalks), plus Support noting the upper 24-inch limit is normally applied where no stop or yield line is used or approach speeds exceed 35 mph. - [Image 3] shows Sections 3C.04 and 3C.05, including the guidance limiting transverse line crosswalks to signal- or STOP/YIELD-controlled locations and the option to use high-visibility designs. - [Image 4] is a table of contents page confirming the location of Chapter 3C (Crosswalk Markings) sections. - [Image 5] shows Section 3H.03/3H.04 and Figure 3H-1, including the guidance on the contrast gap (at least one-half the transverse line width, not less than 6 inches) between white crosswalk lines and aesthetic surface treatments. Citations: - Section 3C.07 Standard §4 (p.592) - Section 3C.04 Guidance §1 (p.591) - Section 3H.03 Guidance §11 (p.623) - Section 3C.05 Option §1 (p.591) - Section 3C.01 Support §1 (p.589) - Section 3C.01 Support §2 (p.589) - Figure 3C-1 (p.590) - Page 631 - Page 632 - Page 17 - Page 664

**Gold answer:** Crosswalk markings must be white. Where transverse lines are used, each line must be at least 6 inches and no more than 24 inches wide. Except for certain ramp-terminal cases, the marked crosswalk should be at least 6 feet wide.

## T035

**Question:** Does satisfying a traffic signal warrant automatically require installation of a traffic control signal?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=0.75, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=No

**Descriptive mean:** 93.8% (Strong partial credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 90%

**Rationale:** The response received 93.8% as the descriptive mean of the four official item scores. The official rubric did not award strict full credit because at least one dimension scored below 1.00. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The response omits or underdevelops these locked elements: it is not conclusive justification. | A warrant is a threshold that triggers analysis Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 4C.01, Section 2B.12, Section 4B.03, Section 4C.08, p.651, p.79. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Installation must be based on an engineering study considering safety, operation, alternatives, and the site. | A signal can also be installed where no warrant is met if an engineering study documents why it is the best solution. Adjudication note: Correct non-compulsion rule; omits the documented installation case where no warrant is met. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.933 and Context Precision@6=0.500. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. The MUTCD expressly states that meeting one or more traffic signal warrants does not by itself require signal installation. Warrant satisfaction is only one factor in an engineering study, and the MUTCD even recommends considering alternatives to signals when warrants are met. A parallel rule applies to all-way stop control warrants. Standards (mandatory): - "The satisfaction of a traffic signal warrant or warrants shall not in itself require the installation of a traffic control signal." - Analogously for unsignalized intersections: "The satisfaction of an all-way stop control warrant or warrants shall not in itself require the installation of all-way stop control at an unsignalized intersection." Guidance (recommended): - Because road user delay and the frequency of some crash types are sometimes higher under signal control than under STOP sign control, consideration should be given to providing alternatives to traffic control signals even if one or more signal warrants has been satisfied. - Where a signal is installed based on projected (estimated) volumes, an engineering study should be done within 1 year of putting the signal into steady (stop-and-go) operation to determine if it is justified (except where Warrant 8 was the basis); if not justified, the signal should be taken out of steady operation or removed. Support (context, non-normative): - The safe and efficient movement of all road users is the primary consideration in the engineering study; installing a signal does not necessarily improve safety and could adversely impact safety at an inappropriate location. Warrant satisfaction is one relevant factor in the engineering study, but not the only factor or the overriding consideration. Agencies can also install a signal where no warrants are met, but only after an engineering study documents the rationale. - The Crash Experience warrant conditions are intended for application where crash severity and frequency are the principal reasons to consider installing a signal — illustrating that warrants frame the study rather than mandate installation. Citations: - Section 4C.01 Standard §3 (p.651) - Section 2B.12 Standard §5 (p.79) - Section 4B.03 Guidance §1 (p.649) - Section 4C.01 Guidance §12 (p.652) - Section 4C.01 Support §20 (p.653) - Section 4C.08 Support §1 (p.661)

**Gold answer:** No. A warrant is a threshold that triggers analysis; it is not conclusive justification. Installation must be based on an engineering study considering safety, operation, alternatives, and the site. A signal can also be installed where no warrant is met if an engineering study documents why it is the best solution.

Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 4A.03, Section 4F.01, p.644, p.682. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The driver must yield to pedestrians lawfully in an associated crosswalk, vehicles lawfully in the intersection, and for left/U-turns, opposing vehicles close enough to be an imme… | The driver may generally proceed straight, turn right or left, or make a U-turn unless another control modifies the movement. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A driver facing a steady CIRCULAR GREEN signal is permitted to proceed straight through, turn right or left, or make a U-turn, unless the movement is modified by other traffic control devices. The driver must yield to pedestrians lawfully within an associated crosswalk and to other vehicles lawfully within the intersection; additionally, drivers turning left or making a U-turn to the left must yield to oncoming vehicles close enough to constitute an immediate hazard. Standards (mandatory): - "Vehicular traffic facing a CIRCULAR GREEN signal indication is permitted to proceed straight through or turn right or left or make a U-turn movement except as such movement is modified by lane-use signs, turn prohibition signs, lane markings, roadway design, separate turn signal indications, or other traffic control devices." - "Such vehicular traffic, including vehicles turning right or left or making a U-turn movement, shall yield the right-of-way to: (a) Pedestrians lawfully within an associated crosswalk, and (b) Other vehicles lawfully within the intersection." - "In addition, vehicular traffic turning left or making a U-turn movement to the left shall yield the right-of-way to other vehicles approaching from the opposite direction so closely as to constitute an immediate hazard during the time when such turning vehicle is moving across or within the intersection." Options (permitted): - A steady straight-through GREEN ARROW signal indication may be used instead of a CIRCULAR GREEN in a signal face to discourage wrong-way turns under specific conditions (e.g., approaches intersecting a one-way street or interchange exit ramp, unique geometric design prohibiting turns, or approaches with pre-signals with separately controlled adjacent lanes), even if opposed by a simultaneous permissive left-turn movement. Citations: - Section 4A.03 Standard §1 (p.644) - Section 4F.01 Option §5 (p.682)

**Gold answer:** The driver may generally proceed straight, turn right or left, or make a U-turn unless another control modifies the movement. The driver must yield to pedestrians lawfully in an associated crosswalk, vehicles lawfully in the intersection, and for left/U-turns, opposing vehicles close enough to be an immediate hazard.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The exclusive function of the yellow change interval is to warn approaching traffic that their permission to proceed is being terminated, after which they will be directed to stop (or, for a protected/permissive turn, that the protected movement is ending). Its duration must be determined using engineering practices. The evidence provided contains no provision addressing pre-yellow vehicular countdown displays, so that part of the question cannot be answered from this evidence. Standards (mandatory): - "The duration of the yellow change interval shall be determined using engineering practices." - For pedestrian hybrid beacons: "The duration of the steady yellow change interval shall be determined using engineering practices in accordance with the provisions in Section 4F.17." - For emergency-vehicle hybrid beacons: "The duration of the steady yellow change interval shall be determined using engineering practices in accordance with the provisions in Section 4F.17." Guidance (recommended): - For pedestrian hybrid beacons, a steady yellow change interval should have a minimum duration of 3 seconds and a maximum duration of 6 seconds, with the longer intervals reserved for use on approaches with higher speeds. Support (informational context): - The exclusive function of the yellow change interval is to warn traffic approaching a signalized location that their permission to proceed is being terminated, after which they will be directed to stop, or in the case of a protected/permissive turning movement, that their protected movement is being terminated after which they will need to turn permissively. - For bicycle signal faces, the yellow change interval warns bicyclists that their permission to proceed is being terminated; providing clearance time through the intersection is the purpose of the red clearance interval, not the yellow change interval. Note on pre-yellow countdown displays: The supplied evidence contains no Standard, Guidance, Option, or Support provision concerning pre-yellow vehicular countdown displays. The evidence is insufficient to answer whether such a display may be used. Citations: - Section 4F.17 Support §1 (p.708) - Section 4F.17 Standard §3 (p.708) - Section 4H.11 Support §3 (p.717) - Section 4N.03 Standard §4 (p.743) - Section 4J.03 Standard §10 (p.730) - Section 4J.03 Guidance §11 (p.730)

**Gold answer:** The yellow change interval warns that permission to proceed or a protected movement is ending. Its duration must be determined using engineering practices, with guidance of 3 to 6 seconds. Pre-yellow displays such as vehicular countdowns or flashing green indications are prohibited, except for permitted advance-warning beacons.

## T038

**Question:** What walking speed is generally used to calculate pedestrian clearance time, and what alternative speed may be used with an extended push-button feature?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 4I.06, Section 4K.05, p.724, p.725, p.734. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The general walking speed is 3.5 feet per second. | Up to 4 feet per second may be used to evaluate sufficiency where an extended push-button press provides slower pedestrians a longer clearance time. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.833. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Pedestrian clearance time is generally calculated using a walking speed of 3.5 feet per second, measured from the curb or edge of pavement to at least the far side of the traveled way or a suitable median. Where an extended push button press function is installed so that slower pedestrians can request more time, a faster walking speed of up to 4 feet per second may be used instead. Slower speeds should be used where slower pedestrians or wheelchair users routinely cross. Guidance (recommended): - "Except as provided in Paragraph 8 of this Section, the pedestrian clearance time should be sufficient to allow a pedestrian crossing in the crosswalk who left the curb or edge of pavement at the end of the WALKING PERSON (symbolizing WALK) signal indication to travel at a walking speed of 3.5 feet per second to at least the far side of the traveled way or to a median of sufficient width for pedestrians to wait." - Where pedestrians who walk slower than 3.5 feet per second, or pedestrians who use wheelchairs, routinely use the crosswalk, a walking speed of less than 3.5 feet per second should be considered in determining the pedestrian clearance time. - The total of the walk interval and pedestrian clearance time should be sufficient to allow a pedestrian who left the pedestrian detector (or a location 6 feet behind the curb face or pavement edge if no detector is present) at the beginning of the WALKING PERSON indication to travel at a walking speed of 3 feet per second to the far side of the traveled way or to the median if a two-stage crossing sequence is used; any additional time required should be added to the walk interval. Options (permitted): - A walking speed of up to 4 feet per second may be used to evaluate the sufficiency of the pedestrian clearance time at locations where an extended push button press function has been installed to provide slower pedestrians an opportunity to request and receive a longer pedestrian clearance time; passive pedestrian detection may also be used to automatically adjust the clearance time based on the pedestrian's actual walking speed or actual clearance of the crosswalk. - The additional time provided by an extended push button press to satisfy pedestrian clearance time needs may be added to either the walk interval or the pedestrian change interval. - Pedestrians may be provided with additional features such as increased crossing time, audible beaconing, or a speech push button information message as a result of an extended push button press. Citations: - Section 4I.06 Guidance §7 (p.724) - Section 4I.06 Option §8 (p.724) - Section 4I.06 Option §9 (p.725) - Section 4I.06 Guidance §10 (p.725) - Section 4I.06 Guidance §14 (p.725) - Section 4K.05 Option §1 (p.734)

**Gold answer:** The general walking speed is 3.5 feet per second. Up to 4 feet per second may be used to evaluate sufficiency where an extended push-button press provides slower pedestrians a longer clearance time.

## T039

**Question:** When is a countdown display mandatory on a pedestrian signal head?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 4I.04, Section 9E.12, p.720, p.1105. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: It is mandatory at a crosswalk when the pedestrian change interval is more than 7 seconds. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.667. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A countdown display is mandatory on all pedestrian signal heads used at crosswalks where the pedestrian change interval is more than 7 seconds. Countdown pedestrian signals are also required where a bicycle box extends across more than one motor-vehicle approach lane. Where the pedestrian change interval is 7 seconds or less, a countdown display is optional. Standards (mandatory): - "All pedestrian signal heads used at crosswalks where the pedestrian change interval is more than 7 seconds shall include a pedestrian change interval countdown display in order to inform pedestrians of the number of seconds remaining in the pedestrian change interval." - "The countdown pedestrian signal shall display the number of seconds remaining until the termination of the pedestrian change interval (flashing UPRAISED HAND). Countdown displays shall not be used during the walk interval. Countdown displays shall not be used during the red clearance interval of a concurrent vehicular phase that is ending simultaneously with or after the end of the pedestrian phase." - "Countdown pedestrian signals shall consist of Portland orange numbers that are at least 6 inches in height on a black opaque background. The countdown pedestrian signal shall be located immediately adjacent to the associated UPRAISED HAND (symbolizing DONT WALK) pedestrian signal head indication." - "Countdown pedestrian signals (see Section 4I.04) for the crosswalk or pedestrian crossing movement that crosses the approach shall accompany bicycle boxes that extend across more than one approach lane for motor vehicles. Countdown pedestrian signals used with bicycle boxes shall display the pedestrian change interval countdown without the need for actuation." Options (permitted): - Pedestrian signal heads used at crosswalks where the pedestrian change interval is 7 seconds or less may include a pedestrian change interval countdown display. Citations: - Section 4I.04 Standard §1 (p.720) - Section 4I.04 Standard §6 (p.720) - Section 4I.04 Standard §4 (p.720) - Section 9E.12 Standard §8 (p.1105) - Section 4I.04 Option §2 (p.720)

**Gold answer:** It is mandatory at a crosswalk when the pedestrian change interval is more than 7 seconds.

The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: After a pedestrian actuation, the beacon must display a flashing CIRCULAR YELLOW indication, then a steady CIRCULAR YELLOW, then both steady CIRCULAR RED indications during the pedestrian walk interval, then alternating flashing CIRCULAR RED indications during the pedestrian change interval. When the pedestrian change interval ends, the beacon faces must go dark, and they remain dark between actuations. Standards (mandatory): - "Following an actuation by a pedestrian, a pedestrian hybrid beacon face shall display a flashing CIRCULAR yellow signal indication, followed by a steady CIRCULAR yellow signal indication, followed by both steady CIRCULAR RED signal indications during the pedestrian walk interval, followed by alternating flashing CIRCULAR RED signal indications during the pedestrian change interval (see Figure 4J-3). Upon … termination of the pedestrian change interval, the pedestrian hybrid beacon faces shall revert to a dark (not illuminated) condition." - "Pedestrian hybrid beacon indications shall be dark (not illuminated) during periods between actuations." - "A pedestrian hybrid beacon face shall consist of three signal sections, with a CIRCULAR YELLOW signal indication centered below two horizontally-aligned CIRCULAR RED signal indications (see Figure 4J-3)." Guidance (recommended): - If the pedestrian hybrid beacon is coordinated as part of a signal system, it should remain in the dark condition after a pedestrian actuation is received until the point in the background cycle when the predetermined flashing yellow interval needs to begin in order to achieve the appropriate coordinated offset. Options (permitted): - If a minimum dark time between activations has been set on the controller, the beacon may remain dark after a pedestrian actuation until the minimum dark time has been provided. - Per the note to Figure 4J-3, an optional steady red clearance interval may be added after Interval 3 (steady yellow), and an optional short buffer interval (alternating flashing red while the pedestrian signal heads display a steady UPRAISED HAND) may be added after Interval 5. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 4J-3 illustrates the six-step sequence — (1) dark until activated, (2) flashing yellow upon activation, (3) steady yellow, (4) steady red during the pedestrian walk interval, (5) alternating flashing red during the pedestrian change interval, and (6) dark again until activated. [Image 4] Page 769 shows the same Figure 4J-3 in the full page context, including the note about the optional steady red clearance and buffer intervals. [Image 2] Page 770 contains the Section 4J.03 Standard text stating the required indication sequence following actuation. [Image 3] Page 771 shows the beacon face design requirement (yellow section centered below two horizontally-aligned red sections) referenced to Figure 4J-3. [Image 5] Page 20 is the table of contents locating Chapter 4J, Pedestrian Hybrid Beacons. Citations: - Section 4J.03 Standard §2 (p.730) - Section 4J.03 Standard §1 (p.729) - Section 4J.02 Standard §2 (p.728) - Section 4J.03 Guidance §7 (p.730) - Section 4J.03 Option §8 (p.730) - Figure 4J-3 (p.729)

**Gold answer:** Flashing circular yellow, then steady circular yellow, then both steady circular red during the walk interval, then alternating flashing circular red during the pedestrian change interval, followed by return to dark.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Each accessible pedestrian push button must have a vibrotactile arrow on the button itself, with high visual contrast, aligned parallel to the direction of travel on the associated crosswalk, so pedestrians with vision disabilities can identify which crossing the button actuates and align themselves properly. A locator tone is also required in the accessible pedestrian signal equipment to help pedestrians find the vibrotactile arrow and push button. The locator tone may be deactivated during the steady DONT WALK phase only if passive pedestrian detection activates it whenever a pedestrian is within a 12-foot radius. Standards (mandatory): - "Each push button shall clearly indicate by means of a vibrotactile arrow which crosswalk signal is actuated by the push button. Vibrotactile arrows shall be located on the button of the push button assembly, shall have high visual contrast (light on dark or dark on light), and shall be aligned parallel to the direction of travel on the associated crosswalk." - "A locator tone shall be incorporated into the accessible pedestrian signal equipment to help pedestrians with vision disabilities locate the vibrotactile arrow, and the associated push button if a push button is provided." Guidance (recommended): - In temporary traffic control contexts, if a pushbutton is used to provide equivalent TTC information to pedestrians with vision disabilities, it should be equipped with a locator tone to notify pedestrians that a special accommodation is available and to help them locate the pushbutton. Options (permitted): - The push button locator tone may default to a deactivated mode during periods when the steady UPRAISED HAND (symbolizing DONT WALK) indication is displayed, provided a passive pedestrian detection system activates the locator tone whenever a pedestrian is present within a 12-foot radius of the push button (other than when the audible walk indication is active); where pedestrian facilities such as sidewalks are present, the passive detection requirement may be limited to pedestrians on those facilities within the 12-foot radius. Citations: - Section 4K.04 Standard §1 (p.734) - Section 4K.04 Standard §2 (p.734) - Section 4K.04 Support §3 (p.734) - Section 4K.04 Option §5 (p.734) - Section 6C.03 Guidance §8 (p.781) - Section 4K.01 Support §11 (p.731)

**Gold answer:** The push button must have a high-contrast vibrotactile arrow on the button, aligned parallel to travel on the associated crosswalk. A locator tone must be incorporated; normally it is 0.15 seconds or less and repeats at 1-second intervals when the audible walk indication is not active, subject to stated exceptions.

Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: They are normally dark and operate only upon pedestrian actuation, stopping after the predetermined period or after a detected pedestrian clears. | When active, they use 75 rapid-flashing sequences per minute. | All RRFB units for the crosswalk begin and end together. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: RRFBs are normally dark and begin flashing only when actuated by a pedestrian, then stop after a predetermined time (or, with passive detection, after the pedestrian clears the crosswalk). All RRFB units at a given crosswalk must start and stop flashing simultaneously. When activated, they must flash at a rapid rate of 75 flashing sequences per minute, which is a specific exception to the normal beacon flash rate requirements. Standards (mandatory): - "The RRFB shall be normally dark, shall initiate operation only upon pedestrian actuation, and shall cease operation at a predetermined time after the pedestrian actuation or, with passive detection, after the pedestrian clears the crosswalk." - "All RRFB units associated with a given crosswalk (including those with an advance crossing sign, if used) shall, when activated, simultaneously commence operation of their rapid flashing indications and shall cease operation simultaneously." - "When activated, the two yellow indications in each RRFB unit shall flash in a rapidly flashing sequence. As a specific exception to the requirements for the flash rate of beacons provided in Paragraph 3 of Section 4S.01, RRFBs shall use a much faster flash rate and shall provide 75 flashing sequences per minute." Guidance (recommended): - The minimum duration of the predetermined period of RRFB operation following each actuation should be based on the procedures for timing pedestrian clearance times for pedestrian signals (see Section 4I.06). Options (permitted): - A pedestrian-activated and/or bicyclist-activated RRFB may be used to provide supplemental emphasis to pedestrian, school, and trail warning signs at marked crosswalks across uncontrolled approaches. - At intersections with two crosswalks on an uncontrolled approach, post-mounted RRFBs may be installed to face only one direction of travel at the first crosswalk that traffic encounters; when activated, the RRFBs on both approaches shall simultaneously commence and cease their rapid flashing indications. Citations: - Section 4L.03 Standard §1 (p.738) - Section 4L.03 Standard §2 (p.738) - Section 4L.03 Standard §6 (p.738) - Section 4L.03 Guidance §3 (p.738) - Section 4L.01 Option §1 (p.736) - Section 4L.02 Option §11 (p.738)

**Gold answer:** They are normally dark and operate only upon pedestrian actuation, stopping after the predetermined period or after a detected pedestrian clears. All RRFB units for the crosswalk begin and end together. When active, they use 75 rapid-flashing sequences per minute.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The MUTCD establishes that road user and worker safety and accessibility should be an integral and high-priority element of every project involving temporary traffic control, from planning through design and construction. This priority extends to all users — motorists, bicyclists, pedestrians (including those with disabilities), and workers — and TTC operations should minimize inconvenience, delay, and crash potential. No mandatory (Standard) provisions appear in the evidence; the priority is expressed as Guidance. Guidance (recommended): - Road user and worker safety and accessibility in TTC zones should be an integral and high-priority element of every project from planning through design and construction, and maintenance and utility work should be planned and conducted with the safety and accessibility of all motorists, bicyclists, pedestrians (including those with disabilities), and workers being considered at all times; if the TTC zone includes a grade crossing, early coordination with the railroad company or light rail transit agency should take place. - Temporary traffic control operations should minimize the inconvenience, delay, and crash potential to affected traffic, and prior notice should be given to affected public or private agencies, emergency services, businesses, railroad companies or transit agencies, and road users before free movement is infringed upon or blocked. - Among the fundamental principles of TTC: general plans or guidelines should be developed to provide safety for motorists, bicyclists, pedestrians, workers, enforcement/emergency officials, and equipment; the basic safety principles governing permanent roadways and roadsides should also govern the design of TTC zones, routing road users through zones as nearly as possible comparable to normal highway situations; a TTC plan, in detail appropriate to the project's complexity, should be prepared and understood by all responsible parties before the site is occupied, with changes approved by a knowledgeable official; and road user movement should be inhibited as little as practical, avoiding frequent and abrupt geometric changes. Citations: - Section 6A.02 Guidance §1 (p.766) - Section 8A.13 Guidance §7 (p.992) - Section 6A.02 Guidance §2 (p.768) - Section 6A.01 Support §3 (p.765) - Section 8A.13 Support §1 (p.992)

**Gold answer:** Road-user and worker safety and accessibility must be treated as integral, high-priority considerations from planning through design and construction, including motorists, bicyclists, pedestrians with disabilities, workers, and coordination with rail transit where applicable.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: All workers within the right-of-way in a TTC zone — including emergency responders — must wear high-visibility safety apparel meeting ANSI/ISEA 107-2015 Performance Class 2 or 3 for both daytime and nighttime activity, with the garment class selected by a person the employer designates as responsible for worker safety. This requirement also applies to uniformed law enforcement directing traffic or handling incidents and to firefighters/emergency responders, though responders and law enforcement may instead wear ANSI/ISEA 207-2006 public safety vests. Class 3 apparel is recommended for flaggers working at night. Standards (mandatory): - "For daytime and nighttime activity, all workers, including emergency responders, within the right-of-way who are within the TTC zone shall wear high-visibility safety apparel that meets the Performance Class 2 or 3 requirements of the ANSI/ISEA 107-2015 publication entitled 'American National Standard for High-Visibility Safety Apparel and Headwear,' or equivalent revisions, except as provided in Paragraph 4 of this Section. A person designated by the employer to be responsible for worker safety shall make the selection of the appropriate class of garment." - "When uniformed law enforcement personnel are used to direct traffic, to investigate crashes, or to handle lane closures, obstructed roadways, and disasters, high-visibility safety apparel as described in this Section shall be worn by the law enforcement personnel." - "Except as provided in Paragraph 6 of this Section, firefighters or other emergency responders working within the right-of-way shall wear high-visibility safety apparel as described in this Section." Guidance (recommended): - For flagger wear during nighttime activity, apparel meeting the Performance Class 3 requirements of ANSI/ISEA 107-2015 (or equivalent revision), and labeled as meeting the ANSI 107-2015 standard performance for Class 3 risk exposure, should be worn. - In traffic incident management contexts, all on-scene responders and news media personnel should constantly be aware of their visibility to oncoming traffic and wear high-visibility apparel. Options (permitted): - Emergency and incident responders and law enforcement personnel within the TTC zone may wear high-visibility safety apparel meeting the performance requirements of ANSI/ISEA 207-2006 ("American National Standard for High-Visibility Public Safety Vests"), or equivalent revisions, and labeled as ANSI 207-2006, in lieu of ANSI/ISEA 107-2015 apparel. Citations: - Section 6C.05 Standard §1 (p.782) - Section 6C.05 Standard §3 (p.782) - Section 6C.05 Standard §5 (p.782) - Section 6C.05 Guidance §7 (p.783) - Section 6O.01 Guidance §7 (p.854) - Section 6C.05 Option §4 (p.782)

**Gold answer:** Workers, including emergency responders, within the right-of-way and TTC zone must wear ANSI/ISEA 107-2015 Performance Class 2 or 3 apparel, or an equivalent revision, subject to specified responder exceptions. The employer's designated safety person selects the appropriate class.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Flagger qualifications are addressed as Guidance, not a mandatory Standard. Because flaggers are responsible for public safety and have more public contact than any other highway workers, they should be trained in proper traffic control practices and public contact techniques, and should be able to satisfactorily demonstrate five specific abilities relating to communication, mobility, device control, applying traffic control practices, and hazard recognition. Guidance (recommended): - Flaggers should be trained in proper traffic control practices and public contact techniques. - Ability to receive and communicate specific instructions clearly, firmly, and courteously. - Ability to move and maneuver quickly in order to avoid danger from errant vehicles. - Ability to control signaling devices (such as paddles and flags) in order to provide clear and positive guidance to drivers approaching a TTC zone in frequently changing situations. - Ability to understand and apply proper traffic control practices, sometimes in stressful or emergency situations. - Ability to recognize dangerous traffic situations and warn workers in sufficient time to avoid injury. - Related guidance: if manual traffic control is needed, it should be provided by qualified flaggers or uniformed law enforcement officers. Citations: - Section 6D.01 Guidance §1 (p.783) - Section 6O.01 Guidance §16 (p.855)

**Gold answer:** A flagger should communicate instructions clearly and courteously; move quickly to avoid danger; control paddles or flags to give clear guidance; understand and apply traffic-control practices under stress; and recognize dangerous situations and warn workers in time.

## T046

Completeness rationale: The audit found incomplete coverage even though the automated clause comparison did not isolate a single missing sentence. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 6H.17, Section 6E.01, Section 6N.14, Section 6E.02, Section 6E.05, Section 6N.09. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Movements from both ends must be coordinated, except for a short, low-volume, mutually visible work zone that can self-regulate. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.947 and Context Precision@6=0.500. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: When traffic in both directions must use a single lane for a limited distance, movements from each end of the section must be coordinated, except in the limited self-regulating case. Permitted control methods include flagger control (one or two flaggers), temporary traffic control signals, and — on short, low-volume worksites with full mutual visibility — self-regulation. Where two-way traffic operates on one roadway of a normally-divided highway, opposing traffic must be physically separated; markings and signing alone are prohibited. Standards (mandatory): - "Except as provided in Paragraph 4 of this Section, when traffic in both directions must use a single lane for a limited distance, movements from each end shall be coordinated." - "When two-lane, two-way traffic control must be maintained on one roadway of a normally-divided highway, opposing vehicular traffic shall be separated with either temporary traffic barriers (concrete safety-shape or approved alternate), channelizing devices, Narrow Two-Way Traffic (W6-4) signs on flexible supports (see Section 6H.17), or a temporary raised island throughout the length of the two-way operation. The use of markings and complementary signing, by themselves, shall not be used." Options (permitted): - When the one-lane, two-way TTC zone is short enough for a flagger to see from one end to the other, traffic may be controlled by either a single flagger or by a flagger at each end of the section. - On a low-volume street or road with a short work space, where road users from both directions can see traffic approaching from the opposite direction through and beyond the worksite, movement through the constriction may be self-regulating. - Traffic control signals may be used to control vehicular traffic movements in one-lane, two-way TTC zones. Citations: - Section 6E.01 Standard §1 (p.787) - Section 6N.14 Standard §2 (p.850) - Section 6E.02 Option §2 (p.787) - Section 6E.01 Option §4 (p.787) - Section 6E.05 Option §1 (p.788) - Section 6N.09 Support §6 (p.846)

**Gold answer:** Movements from both ends must be coordinated, except for a short, low-volume, mutually visible work zone that can self-regulate. Methods include flaggers, flag transfer, a pilot car, temporary traffic signals, or stop/yield control.

Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The assembly or S5-1 sign must be at or as near as practicable to the point where the reduced zone begins. | A School (S1-1) sign must be placed in advance of the first school-speed-limit sign in each direction. | The downstream end is normally marked with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign, with an END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) allowed when the two zones end together. **Model answer:** Direct Answer: The School Speed Limit assembly (or S5-1 sign) must be placed at, or as near as practicable to, the point where the reduced school speed limit zone begins. A School (S1-1) sign is required in advance of the first School Speed Limit assembly or S5-1 sign encountered in each direction. The downstream end of the zone is normally marked with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign, although an END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) sign is permitted if the reduced speed zone ends at the same point as a designated school zone. Standards (mandatory): - "A School Speed Limit assembly (see Figure 7B-1) or a School Speed Limit When Flashing (S5-1) sign (see Figure 7B-1) shall be used to indicate the speed limit where a reduced school speed limit zone has been established based upon an engineering study or where a reduced school speed limit is specified for such areas by statute. The School Speed Limit assembly or School Speed Limit When Flashing sign shall be placed at or as near as practicable to the point where the reduced school speed limit zone begins (see Figures 7B-2 and 7B-4)." - "If a reduced school speed limit zone has been established, a School (S1-1) sign shall be installed in advance (see Table 2C-3 for advance placement guidelines) of the first School Speed Limit sign assembly or S5-1 sign that is encountered in each direction as traffic approaches the reduced school speed limit zone (see Figures 7B-2 and 7B-4)." - "Except as provided in Paragraph 4 of this Section, the downstream end of an authorized and posted reduced school speed limit zone shall be identified with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign (see Figures 7B-1, 7B-2, and 7B-4)." - "The static School Speed Limit assembly shall consist of a top plaque (S4-3P) with the legend SCHOOL, a Speed Limit (R2-1) sign, and a bottom plaque (S4-1P, S4-2P, S4-4P, or S4-6P) indicating the specific periods of the day and/or days of the week that the special school speed limit is in effect (see Figure 7B-1)." - "The School Speed Limit assembly shall be either a static sign assembly, a blank-out sign, or a changeable message sign (see Chapter 2L)." Options (permitted): - "The downstream end of a designated school zone may be identified with an END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) sign (see Figures 7B-1 and 7B-2)." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 7B-2 shows the School (S1-1) sign placed in advance of the School Speed Limit assembly (S4-3P/R2-1/S4-1P) at the beginning of the zone, with the END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) or END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) sign at the downstream end. - [Image 2] Figure 7B-4 similarly shows the S1-1 advance sign, the School Speed Limit assembly at the zone's beginning point (with higher fines plaques), and the S5-3 or S5-2 sign marking the downstream end. - [Image 3] Figure 7B-1 illustrates the components of the School Speed Limit assembly (S4-3P SCHOOL plaque, R2-1 Speed Limit sign, and time-period bottom plaques) as well as the S5-3 and S5-2 end signs. - [Image 4] Page 1019 contains Section 7B.05 Standards requiring placement of the assembly at the beginning of the zone, the advance S1-1 sign, and the S5-3 sign at the downstream end. - [Image 5] Page 1021 contains the Standard specifying the static assembly composition (S4-3P, R2-1, and a bottom plaque) and that the assembly shall be static, blank-out, or changeable message. - [Image 6] Page 1014 contains the Option that the downstream end of a designated school zone may be identified with an END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) sign. - [Image 7] Page 1013 shows Figure 7B-1 (Revision 1) with the School Speed Limit assembly and end-of-zone signs. Citations: - Section 7B.05 Standard §1 (p.978) - Section 7B.05 Standard §2 (p.978) - Section 7B.05 Standard §3 (p.978) - Section 7B.05 Standard §7 (p.980) - Section 7B.05 Standard §6 (p.980) - Section 7B.02 Option §7 (p.976) - Figure 7B-2 (p.975) - Figure 7B-4 (p.979) - Figure 7B-1 (p.973) - Page 1019 - Page 1021 - Page 1014 - Page 1013

**Gold answer:** The assembly or S5-1 sign must be at or as near as practicable to the point where the reduced zone begins. A School (S1-1) sign must be placed in advance of the first school-speed-limit sign in each direction. The downstream end is normally marked with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign, with an END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) allowed when the two zones end together.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A bicycle box must be at least 10 feet deep (measured from the motor vehicle stop line edge to the edge nearest the crosswalk or intersection) and must contain at least one bicycle symbol marking. Green-colored pavement is optional, but if used it is restricted to specific bicycle facility applications (including bicycle boxes) and must cover the full limits of the bicycle box. The evidence provided does not address other operational restrictions (such as signal phasing or turn-on-red limitations) for bicycle boxes. Standards (mandatory): - "If used, the distance from the upstream edge of the bicycle box that is nearest to the stop line for motor vehicles to the downstream edge of the bicycle box that is nearest the crosswalk or intersection shall be at least 10 feet. At least one bicycle symbol marking (see Figure 9E-12) shall be used in the bicycle box." - "If used, green-colored pavement shall be used in the full limits of the bicycle box." - "If used, green-colored pavement shall be limited to: A. Bicycle lanes..., B. Extensions of bicycle lanes through intersections..., C. Extensions of bicycle lanes through areas where motor vehicles enter a mandatory turn lane in which motor vehicles must weave across bicyclists in bicycle lanes..., D. Two-stage bicycle turn boxes..., E. Bicycle Boxes (see Section 9E.12), and F. As a background for bicycle detector symbols..." Guidance (recommended): - If green-colored pavement is used within separated bicycle lanes on an independent alignment, it should be used only at the entrances to those facilities from roadways open to public travel or at conflict, weaving, or crossing locations. (Note: this applies to separated bicycle lanes, not bicycle boxes specifically.) Options (permitted): - Green-colored pavement may be installed for the entire length of a bicycle lane or bicycle lane extension or for only a portion (or portions) of it. (Note: this partial-application flexibility applies to bicycle lanes and lane extensions; within a bicycle box, green pavement must cover the full limits of the box.) Citations: - Section 9E.12 Standard §6 (p.1105) - Section 9E.12 Standard §13 (p.1106) - Section 3H.06 Standard §2 (p.626) - Section 3H.06 Guidance §5 (p.626) - Section 3H.06 Option §7 (p.626) - Section 3H.06 Support §1 (p.625)

**Gold answer:** The box must be at least 10 feet from its upstream edge near the motor-vehicle stop line to its downstream edge near the crosswalk or intersection, must include at least one bicycle symbol, and turns on red must be prohibited from the lane containing the box. If green pavement is used, it must fill the full limits of the bicycle box.

Table 2A-1 assigns: "Octagon* Stop (R1-1)** … Circle* Grade Crossing Advance Warning (W10-1)** Pennant (Isosceles Triangle with longer axis horizontal, pointed right)* No Passing Zone (W14-3)**" - Per the table's footnotes: "* This shape shall be limited exclusively to the sign(s) indicated." and "** This sign shall be exclusively the shape shown." Guidance (recommended): - "Shapes that are exclusive to a particular sign (such as an octagon for STOP, a pennant for NO PASSING ZONE, or a circle for Railroad Advance) should not be obscured by another sign mounted on the back of the same assembly protruding or extending beyond the edge of the sign with the exclusive shape." Recommended alternatives include separate mountings, increasing the size of the exclusive-shape sign, or increasing its mounting height. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Table 2A-1 lists the shape-to-sign pairings, matching Octagon to Stop (R1-1), Circle to Grade Crossing Advance Warning (W10-1), and Pennant to No Passing Zone (W14-3). [Image 2] shows the full page containing Section 2A.05 and Table 2A-1 in context. [Image 4] shows Section 2C.53, which confirms the NO PASSING ZONE (W14-3) sign "shall be a pennant-shaped isosceles triangle with its longer axis horizontal and pointing to the right." Citations: - Section 2A.05 Standard §1 (p.42) - Table 2A-1 (p.42) - Section 2A.05 Guidance §3 (p.42) - Page 83 - Page 231

**Gold answer:** Octagon: STOP; circle: Grade Crossing Advance Warning; pennant: NO PASSING ZONE.

Options (permitted): - "Warning signs used for TTC incident management situations may have a black legend and border on a fluorescent pink background." - "Guide signs used in TTC incident management situations may have a black legend and border on a fluorescent pink background." - "Detour signs in TTC incident management situations may have a black legend and border on a fluorescent pink background." - "Warning and guide signs used for TTC traffic incident management situations may have a black legend and border on a fluorescent pink background (see Figure 6O-1)." Support: - "Common uses of sign colors are shown in Table 2A-2. Color schemes on specific signs are shown in the illustrations located in each applicable Chapter." Visual evidence: [Image 4] shows Table 2A-2, in which "Temporary Traffic Control" is marked with a black legend and orange background, "Incident Management" is marked with a black legend and both orange and fluorescent pink backgrounds, and "ETC-Account Only" is marked with a black legend and a purple background (footnote 5 notes purple use is restricted per Chapter 2F). **Model answer:** Direct Answer: Table 2A-5 shows that white-on-red signs must maintain a white retroreflectivity of at least 35 and a red retroreflectivity of at least 7 (in cd/lx/m², measured at a 0.2° observation angle and -4.0° entrance angle), and these values apply across all sheeting types. In addition, footnote 4 requires a minimum sign contrast ratio of at least 3:1, computed as white retroreflectivity divided by red retroreflectivity. Agencies are required to use an assessment or management method designed to keep signs at or above these Table 2A-5 minimums. Standards (mandatory): - "Public agencies or officials having jurisdiction shall use an assessment or management method that is designed to maintain sign retroreflectivity at or above the minimum levels in Table 2A-5." Guidance (recommended): - Except for signs identified in Paragraph 5, one or more of the methods described in "Maintaining Traffic Sign Retroreflectivity" (FHWA-SA-07-020, Revised 2013), or a method developed based on an engineering study, should be used to maintain sign retroreflectivity at or above the minimum levels in Table 2A-5; signs identified as being below the minimum levels should be replaced. Options (permitted): - Table 2A-5 values for White on Red: "W ≥ 35; R ≥ 7" (a single set of values spanning all beaded and prismatic sheeting types), with Additional Criteria footnote 4. - Footnote 4 of Table 2A-5: "Minimum sign contrast ratio ≥ 3:1 (white retroreflectivity ÷ red retroreflectivity)"; footnote 1 notes the levels are "in units of cd/lx/m² measured at an observation angle of 0.2° and an entrance angle of -4.0°." Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Table 2A-5 with the "White on Red" row listing "W ≥ 35; R ≥ 7" across all sheeting types and referencing footnote 4, which defines the minimum sign contrast ratio ≥ 3:1 (white retroreflectivity ÷ red retroreflectivity). Citations: - Section 2A.22 Standard §2 (p.62) - Section 2A.22 Guidance §4 (p.62) - Section 2A.22 Option §3 (p.63) - Section 2A.22 Option §4 (p.63) - Table 2A-5 (p.62)

**Gold answer:** White at least 35 cd/lx/m², red at least 7 cd/lx/m², and a white-to-red contrast ratio of at least 3:1.

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 5A.03, Table 5A-1, page 760, p.760, Page 801. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: At Level 3, the ADS performs the dynamic driving task within its operational design domain with the expectation that the human driver will respond appropriately to a request to in… | At Level 4, the ADS performs the task even if the human driver does not respond appropriately. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Per Table 5A-1, at Level 3 the Automated Driving System (ADS) performs all aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task within a given Operational Design Domain (ODD) "with the expectation that the human driver will respond appropriately to a request to intervene." At Level 4, the ADS continues sustained performance of the Dynamic Driving Task "even if a human driver does not respond appropriately to a request to intervene." In short, Level 3 still depends on a fallback-ready human driver, while Level 4 does not. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Table 5A-1 shows Level 3 defined as "The driving mode specific sustained performance by an ADS of all aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task within a given ODD with the expectation that the human driver will respond appropriately to a request to intervene," and Level 4 as "The driving mode specific sustained performance by an ADS of all aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task, even if a human driver does not respond appropriately to a request to intervene," with both levels grouped under the "Automated Driving System (ADS)" category. [Image 2] shows the same Table 5A-1 in context on page 760 along with the SAE J3016-based definitions (Items A–G) that support it, confirming that ADS "is used specifically to describe a Level 3, 4, or 5 driving automation system." Support (informational): This table content is presented as Support material derived from SAE J3016; it is definitional, not a normative Standard, Guidance, or Option provision. - "Automation Levels – The levels of automation that are described in Table 5A-1." - "Automated Driving System (ADS) – The hardware and software that are collectively capable of performing the entire Dynamic Driving Task (DDT) on a sustained basis, regardless of whether it is limited to a specific Operational Design Domain (ODD); this term is used specifically to describe a Level 3, 4, or 5 driving automation system." Citations: - Table 5A-1 (p.760) - Section 5A.03 Support §1 (p.760) - Page 801

**Gold answer:** At Level 3, the ADS performs the dynamic driving task within its operational design domain with the expectation that the human driver will respond appropriately to a request to intervene. At Level 4, the ADS performs the task even if the human driver does not respond appropriately.

Table 6B-1 contains information regarding the spacing of advance warning signs. Where a series of two or more advance warning signs is used, the closest sign to the transition and activity areas should be placed approximately 100 feet for low-speed urban streets to 1,000 feet or more for freeways and expressways." - "Typical distances for placement of advance warning signs on freeways and expressways should be longer because drivers are conditioned to uninterrupted flow. Therefore, the advance warning sign placement should extend on these facilities as far as ½ mile or more." - "The distances contained in Table 6B-1 are approximate, are intended for guidance purposes only, and should be applied with engineering judgment. These distances should be adjusted for field conditions, if necessary, by increasing or decreasing the recommended distances." Options (permitted): - The Table 6B-1 text (reproduced with Section 6B.05 §3) lists: "Expressway / Freeway 1,000 feet 1,500 feet 2,640 feet," with the note that "The A dimension is the distance from the transition or point of restriction to the first sign. The B dimension is the distance between the first and second signs. The C dimension is the distance between the second and third signs." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] shows Table 6B-1, with the Expressway/Freeway row listing A = 1,000 feet, B = 1,500 feet, and C = 2,640 feet, along with footnotes defining the A, B, and C dimensions. - [Image 2] shows the full page containing Table 6B-1 (Recommended Advance Warning Sign Minimum Spacing) with the same Expressway/Freeway values. - [Image 3] shows Figure 6P-42 (Work in the Vicinity of an Exit Ramp), where the A, B, and C dimensions are depicted for advance warning sign placement, referring to Table 6B-1 for their meanings. - [Image 4] shows Section 6H.02, whose Guidance references Table 6B-1 for advance warning sign spacing on freeways and expressways. - [Image 5] shows Figure 6P-37 (Double Lane Closure on a Freeway), illustrating the A, B, and C sign spacing dimensions on a freeway with a note referring to Table 6B-1.
